This proves <a href="#ker:shell-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:shell-tail">[ker:shell-tail]</a>. The base region $|p|+|k|\leq2$ is bounded by the same geometric lemma and the total tilted mass. Summing the dyadic shells proves <a href="#ker:sheet-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:sheet-gain">[ker:sheet-gain]</a> and also the finiteness of <a href="#ker:normalized-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:normalized-kernel">[ker:normalized-kernel]</a>. ◻

</details>

The sheet gain bound controls total normalized mass. We next control its velocity tails and its behavior as the outgoing velocity approaches the axis. The key distinction is that the hot input can concentrate transversely on the axis while its axial marginal remains uniformly non-atomic.

<a id="stmt-lem-kernel-tightness"></a>
<div id="lem:kernel-tightness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Uniform tightness and axis estimates). *Fix $L<\infty$. The following conclusions hold uniformly for $c\in\mathcal S_L$ and for outgoing velocities off the axis.*

<a id="ker:uniform-tail"></a>
<a id="ker:large-output"></a>
1.  *The tails in $|p|+|\xi-v|$ are uniformly negligible: $$\label{ker:uniform-tail}
     \lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{c\in\mathcal S_L}\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_v
     \mathcal K_c(v;|p|+|\xi-v|>R)=0.$$ Also, $$\label{ker:large-output}
     \lim_{M\to\infty}\sup_{c\in\mathcal S_L}
     \mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{|v|>M}\mathcal K_c(v;\mathbb R^3\times\mathbb R^3)=0.$$*

<a id="ker:axis-input"></a>
<a id="ker:axis-mismatch"></a>
2.  *For every fixed $M,\delta>0$, $$\begin{aligned}
     &\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
     \sup_{c\in\mathcal S_L}
     \mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{\substack{|v|\leq M\\0<|\bar v|\leq\varepsilon}}
     \mathcal K_c(v;|\bar\xi|\geq\delta)=0,
     \label{ker:axis-input}\\
     &\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
     \sup_{c\in\mathcal S_L}
     \mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{\substack{|v|\leq M\\0<|\bar v|\leq\varepsilon}}
     \mathcal K_c(v;|v_3-p_3|\geq\delta)=0.
     \label{ker:axis-mismatch}
    \end{aligned}$$*

<a id="ker:axial-interval"></a>
<a id="ker:axial-modulus"></a>
3.  *The axial input marginals are uniformly non-atomic. More precisely, for each $R\geq1$ and each interval $I$ of length $0<\ell\leq1$, $$\label{ker:axial-interval}
     \mathcal K_c(v;|p|+|\xi-v|\leq R,\ \xi_3\in I)
     \leq C_{q,m,L,R}\bigl(\ell+\ell^{2-q}\bigr).$$ In combination with <a href="#ker:uniform-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:uniform-tail">[ker:uniform-tail]</a>, this gives $$\label{ker:axial-modulus}
     \lim_{\ell\downarrow0}\sup_{c\in\mathcal S_L}\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_v
     \sup_{|I|\leq\ell}\mathcal K_c(v;\xi_3\in I)=0.$$*

<a id="ker:off-axis-small-input"></a>
4.  *Let $\mathcal S_L(\varepsilon)$ denote the subclass whose elementary transverse widths are at most $\varepsilon$. For every $M<\infty$ and $r_0>0$, $$\label{ker:off-axis-small-input}
     \lim_{\delta\downarrow0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
     \sup_{c\in\mathcal S_L(\varepsilon)}
     \mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{\substack{|v|\leq M\\|\bar v|\geq r_0}}
     \mathcal K_c(v;|\bar\xi|<\delta)=0.$$*

*All these conclusions hold under uniformly bounded sheet amplitudes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-kernel-tightness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-kernel-tightness</summary>

*Proof.* The dyadic estimate <a href="#ker:shell-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:shell-tail">[ker:shell-tail]</a> proves <a href="#ker:uniform-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:uniform-tail">[ker:uniform-tail]</a>. We may consequently work on $|p|+|k|\leq R$ and remove that restriction at the end of each argument. On this region the exponential and polynomial factors in <a href="#ker:energy-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:energy-factor">[ker:energy-factor]</a> are bounded by a constant depending on $R$.

<a id="ker:disk-singularity"></a>
For <a href="#ker:large-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:large-output">[ker:large-output]</a>, if $|\bar v|>2R+1$, then $|\bar v-\bar p|\geq|\bar v|/2$ and $|\bar\xi|\geq|\bar v|-R$. The transverse line estimate, followed by the axial integration over a length at most $2R$, gives $C_R/|\bar v|$. If $|v_3|>2R+1$, integrate the delta in $\xi_3$ instead. Its divisor is $|v_3-p_3|\geq|v_3|/2$, and $$\label{ker:disk-singularity}
 \sup_{a\in\mathbb R^2}\int_{|\zeta-a|\leq R}
       (1+|\zeta|^{-q})\,\,\mathrm d\zeta
 \leq C_q(R^2+R^{2-q}).$$ For completeness, if $|a|\leq2R$ the disk is contained in the disk of radius $3R$ about zero; if $|a|>2R$, then $|\zeta|>|a|/2>R$ on the disk, giving the same bound. We obtain $C_R/|v_3|$. At least one of the two outgoing components becomes large with $|v|$, proving <a href="#ker:large-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:large-output">[ker:large-output]</a>.

For <a href="#ker:axis-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:axis-input">[ker:axis-input]</a>, on $|\bar\xi|\geq\delta$ the factor $W(\xi)$ is bounded by $1+\delta^{-q}$. The regular line estimate and <a href="#ker:transverse-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:transverse-expectation">[ker:transverse-expectation]</a> therefore give $$C_{R,\delta}\frac{|\bar v|^{-1}}{1+|\bar v|^{-q}}
 \leq C_{R,\delta}|\bar v|^{q-1}
 \qquad(0<|\bar v|\leq1).$$ For <a href="#ker:axis-mismatch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:axis-mismatch">[ker:axis-mismatch]</a>, integrate in $\xi_3$ first, using $|v_3-p_3|^{-1}\leq\delta^{-1}$ and <a href="#ker:disk-singularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:disk-singularity">[ker:disk-singularity]</a>. The bound is $C_{R,\delta}/W(v)\leq C_{R,\delta}|\bar v|^q$. These are uniform in the axial law. For <a href="#ker:axial-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:axial-interval">[ker:axial-interval]</a>, discard the bounded exponential and polynomial factors and apply <a href="#ker:geometric-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:geometric-interval">[ker:geometric-interval]</a>. This also proves <a href="#ker:axial-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:axial-modulus">[ker:axial-modulus]</a> after the tail removal.

It remains to prove <a href="#ker:off-axis-small-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:off-axis-small-input">[ker:off-axis-small-input]</a>. Fix the truncation radius $R$. The part $|\bar p|>r_0/4$ is uniformly negligible as the transverse widths tend to zero. Indeed, $$\mathbf 1_{|\bar p|>r_0/4}\mathcal G_d(\,\mathrm d\bar p)
 \leq2e^{-r_0^2/(32d^2)}\mathcal G_{\sqrt2d}(\,\mathrm d\bar p),$$ and Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> applies to the wider Gaussian for all sufficiently small $d$. Now retain $|\bar p|\leq r_0/4$. With the notation of Lemma <a href="#ker:geometric-slice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ker:geometric-slice">4</a>, $$|n|\geq\tfrac34r_0,\qquad
 A=\frac{(\bar v-\bar p)\cdot\bar v}{|\bar v-\bar p|}
     \geq\tfrac35r_0.$$ If $|v_3-p_3|\leq r_0^2/(8R)$, then for $|\xi_3-v_3|\leq R$, $$|B(\xi_3-v_3)|\leq\tfrac16r_0.$$ The transverse collision line stays at distance greater than $r_0/3$ from zero, and cannot meet $|\bar\xi|<\delta$ when $\delta<r_0/3$. In the complementary case, $|v_3-p_3|^{-1}\leq8R/r_0^2$. Integrating first in $\xi_3$ bounds the small-input contribution by $$C_{R,r_0}\int_{|\bar\xi|<\delta}
               (1+|\bar\xi|^{-q})\,\,\mathrm d\bar\xi
 \leq C_{R,r_0}(\delta^2+\delta^{2-q}).$$ First let the widths tend to zero, then $\delta$ tend to zero, and finally remove the truncation. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Gaussian parameter laws and compressed axial maps

The next formulation records the uniformity needed when a cold profile is parameterized before its axial change of variables.

<a id="stmt-ker-parameter-sheets"></a>
<div id="ker:parameter-sheets" class="lemma">

<a id="ker:parameter-map"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Gaussian parameter tails). *Let a measure in parameters $w=(\bar w,w_3)\in\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb R$ have density $j$ satisfying $$0\leq j(w)\leq C_0\pi^{-3/2}e^{-|w|^2}.$$ Let its velocity image be given by $$\label{ker:parameter-map}
 \bar p=d\bar w,\qquad p_3=\Phi(w_3),\qquad
 0\leq d\leq\tfrac1{16},\qquad
 |\Phi(a)|\leq A+\delta|a|,\quad0\leq\delta\leq\tfrac1{16},$$ where $\Phi$ is merely measurable. The image measure is dominated by $C_0$ times a sheet in $\mathcal S_{L_A}$, with $L_A$ depending only on $A$. Its gain satisfies Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> with a constant depending only on $q,m,A,C_0$.*

<a id="ker:parameter-tail"></a>
*If $c_{>B}$ is the image of the restricted parameter measure $\mathbf 1_{|w|>B}j(w)\,\,\mathrm dw$, then $$\label{ker:parameter-tail}
 \mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_v\frac{P(c_{>B},H)(v)}{H(v)}
 \leq C_{q,m,A,C_0}e^{-B^2/2},\qquad B\geq0.$$ Thus parameter truncations are valid in the normalized collision measure, even when $\Phi$ compresses intervals or has atoms in its image law. Probability mixtures of measurable families with common $q,m,A,C_0$ and the width bounds in <a href="#ker:parameter-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:parameter-map">[ker:parameter-map]</a> preserve these assertions. More generally, a positive mixing measure of total mass $B_0<\infty$ multiplies the domination amplitude and quantitative upper bounds by $B_0$; the sheet parameter $L_A$ is unchanged. For <a href="#ker:parameter-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:parameter-tail">[ker:parameter-tail]</a>, impose the restriction $|w|>B$ in each component before mixing. Additional measurable masks taking values in $[0,1]$ preserve the assertions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ker-parameter-sheets"><summary>Proof of stmt-ker-parameter-sheets</summary>

*Proof.* The Gaussian majorant is a product measure. Its transverse image is $\mathcal G_d$, and its axial image is the probability $\rho=\Phi_*(\pi^{-1/2}e^{-a^2}\,\,\mathrm da)$. There is no need to differentiate or invert $\Phi$. From <a href="#ker:parameter-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:parameter-map">[ker:parameter-map]</a>, $$e^{8|\Phi(a)|^2}\leq e^{16A^2}e^{16\delta^2a^2}.$$ Even under the wider axial probability $(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-a^2/2}\,\,\mathrm da$, the integral of the right side is $$e^{16A^2}(1-32\delta^2)^{-1/2}\leq2e^{16A^2}.$$ We may therefore take $L_A=2e^{16A^2}$ for both the original and the wider parameter laws. The original image has the required sheet domination.

On the parameter tail, $$\mathbf 1_{|w|>B}e^{-|w|^2}
 \leq e^{-B^2/2}e^{-|w|^2/2}.$$ After normalization the right side is $2^{3/2}e^{-B^2/2}$ times a Gaussian probability with parameter widths enlarged by $\sqrt2$. Its transverse velocity width is at most $\sqrt2/16<1/8$, and its axial image has the exponential moment just proved. Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> applied to this dominating image gives <a href="#ker:parameter-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:parameter-tail">[ker:parameter-tail]</a>.

For a probability mixture, mix the dominating sheets just constructed; this is again a member of $\mathcal S_{L_A}$. If the mixing measure has mass $B_0>0$, normalize it by $B_0$ and retain that factor in the domination amplitude. If $B_0=0$, the mixed image is zero. The same argument applied to the wider Gaussian majorants of the component tails proves the asserted factor in <a href="#ker:parameter-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:parameter-tail">[ker:parameter-tail]</a>. Thus the estimates are uniform over families of mixtures whose masses $B_0$ have a common finite bound independent of the tail cutoff. Masks taking values in $[0,1]$ only decrease the measures, whether imposed before or after the pushforward. ◻

</details>

For example, suppose an axial velocity is determined by $p_3=a(z-tp_3)+d w_3$, where $a$ is nondecreasing, and $u$ solves $u=a(z-tu)$. The function $p\mapsto p-a(z-tp)$ is increasing with inverse Lipschitz constant at most one. Hence <a id="ker:compressed-displacement"></a>$$\label{ker:compressed-displacement}
 |p_3-u|\leq d|w_3|.$$ If $|u|\leq A$, Lemma <a href="#ker:parameter-sheets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ker:parameter-sheets">8</a> applies. A large Jacobian compression of the axial law does not affect its constants. Gaussian majorants with any fixed positive exponent in place of one are covered by a fixed rescaling of the parameters and a corresponding decrease of the upper bounds on the physical widths.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The hot–hot gain

<a id="stmt-lem-hot-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:hot-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Quadratic gain bound). *There is a constant $C_{q,m}$ such that <a id="ker:hot-gain"></a>$$\label{ker:hot-gain}
 P(H,H)\leq C_{q,m}H
 \quad\hbox{almost everywhere}.$$ In fact the proof below works for every $m>5$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hot-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hot-bound</summary>

<a id="ker:hot-reduced"></a>
*Proof.* The integrand is symmetric in $p,\xi$. On the collision plane, $|v|^2\leq|p|^2+|\xi|^2$, so at least one incoming speed is at least $|v|/2$. It suffices, at a factor of two, to retain $|\xi|\geq|v|/2$. Formula <a href="#ker:energy-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:energy-factor">[ker:energy-factor]</a> now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{P(H,H)(v)}{H(v)}
 &\leq C_m\int_{|\xi|\geq|v|/2}
 \delta\bigl((v-p)\cdot(\xi-v)\bigr)
 e^{-|p+\xi-v|^2}\langle p\rangle^{-m}
 \frac{W(p)W(\xi)}{W(v)}\,\,\mathrm dp\,\,\mathrm d\xi,
 \label{ker:hot-reduced}
\end{aligned}$$ because $\langle v\rangle^m/\langle\xi\rangle^m\leq2^m$.

<a id="ker:hot-shell-weight"></a>
Again write $k=\xi-v$ and $w=p+k$. On $R<|p|+|k|\leq2R$, $R\geq1$, either $|p|>R/4$ or $|w|>R/2$. Consequently $$\label{ker:hot-shell-weight}
 \langle p\rangle^{-m}e^{-|w|^2}
 \leq C_mR^{-m}e^{-|w|^2/2}.$$ In the second alternative this uses the finite supremum of $s^m e^{-s^2/2}$; in the first it follows directly from the polynomial factor.

The measure $W(p)\mathbf 1_{|p|\leq2R}\,\,\mathrm dp$ is dominated by $$\bigl(1+|\bar p|^{-q}\bigr)
 \mathbf 1_{|\bar p|\leq2R}\,\,\mathrm d\bar p
 \ \otimes\ \mathbf 1_{|p_3|\leq2R}\,\,\mathrm dp_3.$$ Its transverse factor is a positive mixture of centered disks. Indeed, for $D>0$, $$|\bar p|^{-q}\mathbf 1_{|\bar p|\leq D}
 =D^{-q}\mathbf 1_{|\bar p|\leq D}
   +q\int_0^D a^{-q-1}\mathbf 1_{|\bar p|\leq a}\,\,\mathrm da$$ almost everywhere. Upon normalizing the disks, the mixture has total mass $\pi D^2+2\pi D^{2-q}/(2-q)$. The axial factor has mass $4R$. For $D=2R$ the resulting product mixture therefore has total mass at most $C_qR^3$.

Discard $e^{-|w|^2/2}$ and the incoming-speed restriction in <a href="#ker:hot-reduced" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:hot-reduced">[ker:hot-reduced]</a>. Apply Lemma <a href="#ker:geometric-slice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ker:geometric-slice">4</a> to each normalized product in this mixture, with $|k|\leq2R$. In conjunction with <a href="#ker:hot-shell-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:hot-shell-weight">[ker:hot-shell-weight]</a>, this yields the shell estimate <a id="ker:hot-shell"></a>$$\label{ker:hot-shell}
 \frac{P(H,H)(v)}{H(v)}\bigg|_{R<|p|+|\xi-v|\leq2R}
 \leq C_{q,m}R^{5-m}.$$ The notation on the left denotes the restricted nonnegative integral from <a href="#ker:hot-reduced" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:hot-reduced">[ker:hot-reduced]</a>, including its fixed symmetry factor. The base region is bounded by the same disk-mixture argument with $R=1$. Summation over dyadic $R$ proves the result for $m>5$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ker-matching-axis"></a>
<div id="ker:matching-axis" class="remark">

*Remark 10* (A sharp axial endpoint). A single normalized gain need not become small as the outgoing velocity approaches the axis. Take $c=\delta_{(0,0,u)}$ and $v_\varepsilon=(\varepsilon,0,u)$. The collision plane is $\xi_1=\varepsilon$, and $$\frac{P(c,H)(v_\varepsilon)}{H(v_\varepsilon)}
 =\frac{2}{\varepsilon H(v_\varepsilon)}
      \int_{\mathbb R^2}H(\varepsilon,y,z)\,\,\mathrm dy\,\,\mathrm dz.$$ The regular part of $W(\varepsilon,y,z)$ contributes $O(\varepsilon^{q-1})$. In its singular part substitute $y=\varepsilon s$. Dominated convergence, with dominating function $(1+s^2)^{-q/2}e^{-z^2}\langle z\rangle^{-m}$, gives $$\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
 \frac{P(c,H)(v_\varepsilon)}{H(v_\varepsilon)}
 =2a_q e^{u^2}\langle u\rangle^m
        \int_{\mathbb R}e^{-z^2}\langle z\rangle^{-m}\,\,\mathrm dz>0.$$ The corresponding axial input marginal converges to the absolutely continuous measure $$2a_q e^{u^2}\langle u\rangle^m
 e^{-z^2}\langle z\rangle^{-m}\,\,\mathrm dz.$$ Thus transverse concentration is compatible with the uniform axial interval estimate. This is the reason for using two successive gains in the axial part of the later nonconcentration argument.

</div>

<a id="jet:section"></a>
### Cold jets and uniform color estimates

We construct the common finite-entropy datum and bound each cold color by its free transport plus a small Maxwellian remainder. Narrow velocity profiles control self-collisions, while spatial gaps make collisions between distinct colors small. These estimates supply the cold density bound and the mixed-cold source bound used to select the hot caps in Section <a href="#sec:cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cap">5</a>.

The estimates in this section hold for every fixed choice $$K\ge1,\qquad 0<\eta\le\log2,\qquad
 0<\alpha\le\tfrac14,\qquad 0<\gamma\le1.$$ These four parameters will subsequently be fixed by the growth argument. Here we choose the geometric and thermal parameters after them. To keep the later shortening of the time interval separate from the estimates, fix the preliminary bounds <a id="jet:preliminary"></a>$$\overline s=\tfrac18,\qquad \overline T=\tfrac1{32},
 \qquad 0<s_0\le\overline s,\qquad 0<T_0\le\overline T .
 \label{jet:preliminary}$$ All estimates proved below hold on the preliminary interval $[0,\overline T]$. Their constants do not change when $T_0$ is subsequently decreased. They may depend on the fixed parameters and on the fixed function $J$, but never on the number of retained annuli, the collision cutoff, or the size of the hot component.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The geometry and the datum

Put $$s_j=s_0e^{-j\eta},\qquad p_j=s_j^2,\qquad
 c_\eta=\frac{e^\eta-1}{16},\qquad
 \delta_j=\frac{c_\eta}{4}s_j^2,\qquad j\ge1.$$ For $s=s_j$, define the annular core and the two stripe patterns by $$\begin{aligned}
 A_j&=\{y\in\mathbb R^2:
       s_j+c_\eta p_j<|y|<e^\eta s_j-c_\eta p_j\},\\
 P_j^+&=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb Z}
       \{y:kp_j<y_1<(k+\tfrac14)p_j\},\\
 P_j^-&=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb Z}
       \{y:(k+\tfrac12)p_j<y_1<(k+\tfrac34)p_j\},\\
 E_j^\sigma&=A_j\cap P_j^\sigma,\qquad \sigma\in\{-1,+1\}.
\end{aligned}$$ Each stripe pattern has period $p_j$ and fraction $\theta=1/4$. This is a statement about the periodic pattern: intersecting it with the annulus need not preserve the fraction exactly. We retain the original sets $E_j^\sigma$ in the datum.

<a id="jet:enlargements"></a>
Let $$\widehat E_j^\sigma
   =\{y:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(y,E_j^\sigma)\le\delta_j\},
 \qquad a=\tfrac18,\qquad b_{\mathrm{sp}}=\tfrac1{16}.$$ For a label $i=(j,\sigma,n)$, $n=(\bar n,n_3)\in\mathbb Z^3$, set $$\mathcal O_i(t)=
 (\widehat E_j^\sigma+\bar n)
 \times[n_3-a-2t-b_{\mathrm{sp}},
        n_3+a+2t+b_{\mathrm{sp}}].
 \label{jet:enlargements}$$ These enlargements will only localize upper bounds; they will not replace the stripe masks when their average is computed.

<a id="stmt-jet-geometry"></a>
<div id="jet:geometry" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Disjoint enlargements). *For $0\le t\le\overline T$, the sets $\mathcal O_i(t)$ are pairwise disjoint. Moreover, $$\widehat E_j^\sigma\subset
 \{s_j<|y|<e^\eta s_j\}\subset B(0,s_0).$$ At each point of the periodic lift there is therefore at most one label whose enlargement contains that point.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-geometry"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-geometry</summary>

*Proof.* Opposite stripe patterns at the same scale have distance at least $p_j/4$, whereas $2\delta_j=c_\eta p_j/2<p_j/4$. The radial gap between the cores for the adjacent scales $s_j$ and $s_{j+1}$ is $$c_\eta(p_j+p_{j+1})
   >\delta_j+\delta_{j+1}.$$ The gaps between nonadjacent cores are larger. Enlarging by $\delta_j<c_\eta p_j$ still leaves the $j$-th set inside its original open annulus. This proves disjointness of all the untranslated transverse enlargements. They lie in $B(0,s_0)$, so their distinct integer translates are disjoint as well. Finally, $$a+2\overline T+b_{\mathrm{sp}}=\tfrac14.$$ The axial intervals belonging to distinct integers $n_3$ are disjoint. These two observations prove the assertion for the products in <a href="#jet:enlargements" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:enlargements">[jet:enlargements]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="jet:thermal-preliminary"></a>
<a id="eq:datum"></a>
Fix a nonnegative smooth probability density $J$, supported in the unit ball of $\mathbb R^3$. Write $$S(\ell)=\operatorname{sech}^2\ell,\qquad
 a_s^\sigma(z)=\sigma+\alpha\tanh(\gamma z/s),
 \qquad d_s=d_0s^{10}.$$ The positive parameter $d_0\le1$ will be decreased below finitely many times. In particular, we require that $$d_0\overline s^{10}\le\tfrac1{64},\qquad
 \overline T d_0\overline s^8\le c_\eta/8 .
 \label{jet:thermal-preliminary}$$ The width $1/64$, including the fixed Gaussian widenings used below, lies within the range of Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a>. For $i=(j,\sigma,n)$, with $s=s_j$, define on the lift $$c_i^0(x,v)=
 \mathbf 1_{E_j^\sigma}(y-\bar n)\mathbf 1_{(-a,a)}(z-n_3)
 \frac K s S\!\left(\frac{\gamma(z-n_3)}s\right)d_s^{-3}
 J\!\left(\frac{v-a_s^\sigma(z-n_3)e_3}{d_s}\right).$$ The periodic datum is $$F_0(x,v)=\sum_{j\ge1}\sum_{\sigma=\pm1}
                 \sum_{n\in\mathbb Z^3}c_{(j,\sigma,n)}^0(x,v).
 \label{eq:datum}$$ At a given spatial point at most one summand is nonzero. Integer translations permute the labels, so the sum is periodic.

<a id="stmt-lem-datum"></a>
<div id="lem:datum" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Integrability of the datum). *The datum <a href="#eq:datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:datum">[eq:datum]</a> satisfies $$\int_\Omega F_0(1+|v|^2+|\log F_0|)\,\,\mathrm dx\,\,\mathrm dv<\infty .$$ Its truncations $F_0^{(N)}$, retaining $j\le N$, converge to $F_0$ in mass, energy, and relative entropy. In addition, if $\chi\ge0$ is a fixed bounded smooth compactly supported function of $(x,v)$, periodized after replacing $x$ by $x/s_N$, and $0\le\lambda_N\le L_0<\infty$, then $$F_0^{(N)}+\lambda_Ns_N\chi(x/s_N,v)$$ has the same limiting mass, energy, and relative entropy as $F_0$. For the last assertion $s_0$ may be decreased so that distinct periodic copies of the spatial support of the seed are disjoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-datum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-datum</summary>

*Proof.* Integration over one period and summation over integer copies equal integration of each unshifted prototype over $\mathbb R^3_x$. The transverse area of $E_j^\sigma$ is at most $C_\eta s_j^2$, and $$\int_{-a}^a S(\gamma z/s)\,\,\mathrm dz
 \le \frac s\gamma\int_\mathbb RS(\ell)\,\,\mathrm d\ell
 =\frac{2s}{\gamma}.$$ The velocity factor integrates to one. Thus the mass of each annular sign prototype is at most $Cs^2$. Its velocities satisfy $$|v|\le1+\alpha+d_s<2,$$ which gives the same bound for its energy.

On the set where the density is positive, decompose its logarithm into the three terms $$\log((K/s)d_s^{-3}),\qquad
 \log S(\gamma z/s),\qquad \log J(w).$$ The first has absolute value at most $C(1+|\log s|)$. The second is integrable against its own weight since $$\int_\mathbb RS(\ell)|\log S(\ell)|\,\,\mathrm d\ell<\infty:$$ on $|\ell|\ge1$, its integrand is bounded by $C(1+|\ell|)e^{-2|\ell|}$, and on a bounded interval it is bounded. Also $\int J|\log J|<\infty$, because $J$ is bounded and compactly supported and $r|\log r|$ is bounded for $0\le r\le1$. Consequently the absolute entropy of an annular sign prototype is at most $$Cs^2(1+|\log s|).$$ The sums of these bounds over the geometric scales converge. Since the spatial supports of the prototypes are disjoint modulo periodicity, the same estimates prove convergence of the truncated mass, energy, and absolute entropy. As $-\log M$ is a constant plus $|v|^2/2$, they prove relative entropy convergence as well.

For the seed $h_N^0=\lambda_Ns_N\chi(x/s_N,v)$, a change of spatial variables gives $$\int h_N^0(1+|v|^2)\le Cs_N^4,\qquad
 \int h_N^0|\log h_N^0|
       \le Cs_N^4(1+|\log s_N|).$$ The constants are uniform in $\lambda_N\in[0,L_0]$, including $\lambda_N=0$, since $\lambda|\log\lambda|$ is bounded on bounded intervals. It remains to check overlap with the cold datum. At every point $$0\le F_0^{(N)}\le C s_N^{-31}.$$ For $f,b\ge0$, integration of $1+\log(f+r)$ over $0<r<b$ gives, with the convention at $b=0$, $$|(f+b)\log(f+b)-f\log f|
 \le b\{2+\log^+(f+b)\}+b|\log b|.$$ Indeed the positive part of the logarithm is bounded by $\log^+(f+b)$, and $\int_0^b\log^-(f+r)\,\,\mathrm dr
 \le\int_0^b\log^-r\,\,\mathrm dr\le b(1+|\log b|)$. On the seed support, $\log^+(F_0^{(N)}+h_N^0)\le C(1+|\log s_N|)$. The entropy perturbation is therefore $O(s_N^4(1+|\log s_N|))$. The mass and Maxwellian-reference terms are controlled by the seed’s mass and energy estimates. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### A transported Maxwellian barrier

<a id="jet:scalar-flow"></a>
<a id="jet:majorant"></a>
For a fixed scale and sign, the equation $$u_s^\sigma(t,z)=a_s^\sigma(z-tu_s^\sigma(t,z))
 \label{jet:scalar-flow}$$ defines $u_s^\sigma$ for all $t\ge0,z\in\mathbb R$. Indeed $\tau\mapsto\tau+t a_s^\sigma(\tau)$ is strictly increasing, has derivative at least one, and maps $\mathbb R$ onto $\mathbb R$. In particular, $|u_s^\sigma|\le1+\alpha$. For label $i=(j,\sigma,n)$, abbreviate $$u_i(t,z)=u_{s_j}^\sigma(t,z-n_3),\quad
 \tau_i=z-n_3-tu_i(t,z),\quad w_i=v-u_i(t,z)e_3,$$ and set $$D_{y,i}=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(y-\bar n,E_j^\sigma),\qquad
 D_{z,i}=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\tau_i,[-a,a]).$$ Choose $c_{\mathrm{sp}}=1/8$ and a fixed sufficiently large $C_m$, depending only on $J$, and define, for $t>0$, $$m_i(t,x,v)=
 C_m\frac K{s_j}d_{s_j}^{-3} e^t
 \exp\left[-\frac{(2-t)|w_i|^2}{d_{s_j}^2}
 -\frac{c_{\mathrm{sp}}(D_{y,i}^2+D_{z,i}^2)}
              {d_{s_j}^2t}\right].
 \label{jet:majorant}$$ Its trace at $t=0$ is defined by the limit along free characteristics. Boundary values of the spatial masks are irrelevant. Denote the free transport of the $i$-th initial color by $$f_i(t,x,v)=c_i^0(x-tv,v).$$

<a id="stmt-jet-barrier"></a>
<div id="jet:barrier" class="lemma">

<a id="jet:barrier-derivative"></a>
<a id="jet:barrier-collision"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Barrier identities). *The constant $C_m$ can be chosen so that $f_i\le m_i$ on $[0,\overline T]$, uniformly in the label. Moreover, almost everywhere along every free characteristic, $$D_tm_i\ge
 \left(1+\tfrac78 |w_i|^2/d_{s_j}^2\right)m_i .
 \label{jet:barrier-derivative}$$ There is a constant $C_\nu$, independent of the label, such that $$P(m_i,m_i)=m_i\nu_{m_i},\qquad
 \nu_{m_i}(v)\le C_\nu\frac K{s_j}(d_{s_j}+|w_i|).
 \label{jet:barrier-collision}$$ Every free core remains in its own spatial enlargement: $$f_i(t,x,v)>0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x\in\mathcal O_i(t).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-barrier"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-barrier</summary>

*Proof.* Suppress the indices, write $d=d_s$, and put $A=(a_s^\sigma)'(\tau)\ge0$. Implicit differentiation gives <a id="jet:flow-identities"></a>$$u_z=\frac A{1+tA}\ge0,\qquad
 u_t=-u u_z,\qquad
 D_tu=(v_3-u)u_z,\qquad
 D_t\tau=\frac{v_3-u}{1+tA}.
 \label{jet:flow-identities}$$ Consequently $$D_t|w|^2=-2u_z w_3^2.$$ The distance functions are Lipschitz, hence absolutely continuous along flights, and at their almost-everywhere differentiability points $$|D_tD_y|\le|\bar w|,\qquad
 |D_tD_z|\le |w_3|/(1+tA)\le|w_3|.$$ Differentiating the logarithm of <a href="#jet:majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:majorant">[jet:majorant]</a> now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 D_t\log m
 ={}&1+\frac{|w|^2}{d^2}
      +\frac{2(2-t)u_z w_3^2}{d^2}\\
    &+\frac{c_{\mathrm{sp}}(D_y^2+D_z^2)}{d^2t^2}
      -\frac{2c_{\mathrm{sp}}(D_yD_tD_y+D_zD_tD_z)}{d^2t}.
\end{aligned}$$ For each of the two spatial terms use $r^2-2r q\ge-q^2$. This yields $$D_t\log m\ge
 1+(1-c_{\mathrm{sp}})|w|^2/d^2
   +2(2-t)u_z w_3^2/d^2.$$ The last term is nonnegative on the preliminary time interval, proving <a href="#jet:barrier-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:barrier-derivative">[jet:barrier-derivative]</a>. In particular the large derivative of the initial axial profile causes no adverse $s^{-1}$ term.

To compare with free transport, start a core flight at $x^0=(y^0,z^0)$ in its initial support and write $$v=a_s^\sigma(z^0-n_3)e_3+d w^0,\qquad |w^0|\le1.$$ At $x=x^0+tv$, the function $p\mapsto p-a_s^\sigma(z-n_3-tp)$ has derivative at least one, its value at $v_3$ is $dw_3^0$, and its value at $u$ is zero. Thus $$|v_3-u|\le d|w_3^0|,\quad |\bar v|=d|\bar w^0|,\quad
 D_y\le td|\bar w^0|,\quad D_z\le td|w_3^0|.$$ In particular $|w|\le d$ and $(D_y^2+D_z^2)/(d^2t)\le t$. The exponential in <a href="#jet:majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:majorant">[jet:majorant]</a>, including $e^t$, is bounded below on this flight by $e^{-3}$. Taking $C_m\ge e^3\|J\|_\infty$ gives $f_i\le m_i$, since $S\le1$. The same calculation supplies the required initial domination. Away from the closed initial spatial support the trace of $m_i$ is zero; on it the distance exponent tends to zero along flights. The inequalities therefore integrate from time zero. No differentiation of a spatial indicator is used.

At a fixed point $(t,x)$, $m_i$ is a scalar multiple of the Maxwellian with center $u_i e_3$ and exponent $(2-t)/d_s^2$. Conservation of momentum and energy in an elastic collision gives $m_i'm_{i,*}'=m_i m_{i,*}$, which proves the first identity in <a href="#jet:barrier-collision" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:barrier-collision">[jet:barrier-collision]</a>. Its velocity mass is at most $CK/s$, and its first moment about $u_i e_3$ is at most $CKd_s/s$, by the substitution $p=u_i e_3+d_s\xi$. The inequality $|v-p|\le|w_i|+|p-u_i e_3|$ proves the frequency bound.

Finally, the free-flight estimates and <a href="#jet:thermal-preliminary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:thermal-preliminary">[jet:thermal-preliminary]</a> give $$D_y\le\overline T d_s\le\delta_j/2.$$ Its axial velocity has modulus less than $2$, so its position lies between $n_3-a-2t$ and $n_3+a+2t$. It belongs to $\mathcal O_i(t)$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Cold control and the density Jacobian

The labels for the finite approximation are $$\mathcal I_N=\{(j,\sigma,n):1\le j\le N,\ \sigma=\pm1,\
                                  n\in\mathbb Z^3\}.$$ For $i=(j,\sigma,n)$ we also write $s_i=s_j$. The infinitely many integer-copy labels merely implement periodicity: translating $x$ permutes them. We consider nonnegative colored mild solutions with these initial colors and any nonnegative hot component, with a common finite collision cutoff $b>0$. The statement below applies throughout every interval on which the finite-approximation solution exists with bounded weighted sup norm. Its constants do not use that norm or any bound on the hot component.

<a id="stmt-prop-cold-control"></a>
<div id="prop:cold-control" class="proposition">

<a id="jet:thermal-comparison"></a>
**Proposition 14** (Uniform cold control). *There is a constant $L$, depending on the fixed parameters, such that after imposing the additional condition $$Ld_0\overline s^9\le1,
 \label{jet:thermal-comparison}$$ the following statements hold at every time of existence of the finite approximation in $0\le t\le\overline T$.*

<a id="jet:cold-remainder"></a>
1.  *For every retained label $i=(j,\sigma,n)$, $$0\le c_i\le f_i+\varepsilon_j m_i,\qquad
      \varepsilon_j=Ld_{s_j}/s_j .
      \label{jet:cold-remainder}$$*

<a id="jet:escaped"></a>
2.  *At each spatial point call the unique label with $x\in\mathcal O_i(t)$, if one exists and is retained, the main label. Let $c_{\mathrm{main}}$ be that color, or zero, and set $e=c-c_{\mathrm{main}}$. For every $A\ge0$, $$0\le e(t,x,v)\le C_A t^A e^{-4|v|^2}.
      \label{jet:escaped}$$ The upper bound remains valid after restricting the escaped sum to any subset of its labels.*

<a id="jet:moment-envelope"></a>
3.  *If the main label has scale $s$, its velocity measure is bounded by a fixed multiple of $(t+s)^{-1}$ times a sum of admissible probability sheets from Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a>. Its free-core contribution has the exact parameterization <a href="#eq:core-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-jacobian">[eq:core-jacobian]</a> below. The remainder has the smaller velocity-mass coefficient $Cd_s/s^2$. In particular, for every fixed $k\ge0$, $$\int_{\mathbb R^3}\langle v\rangle^k c_{\mathrm{main}}\,\,\mathrm dv
           \le\frac{C_k}{t+s},\qquad
      \int_{|v|>R}\langle v\rangle^k c_{\mathrm{main}}\,\,\mathrm dv
           \le\frac{C_k e^{-c_kR^2}}{t+s}\quad(R\ge3).
      \label{jet:moment-envelope}$$*

<a id="jet:mixed-source"></a>
4.  *For the mixed-cold source in the hot equation, $$\sum_{\substack{i,j\in\mathcal I_N\\i\ne j}}
           P^b(c_i,c_j)(t,x,v)
           \le C_A t^{A-1}H(v)\qquad (t>0)
      \label{jet:mixed-source}$$ for every $A\ge0$, with constants uniform in $N$ and $b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-cold-control"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-cold-control</summary>

*Proof.* *Nonlinear comparison.* For one color put $U_i=f_i+\varepsilon_jm_i$. Lemma <a href="#jet:barrier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:barrier">13</a> gives $U_i\le(1+\varepsilon_j)m_i$. By <a href="#jet:thermal-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:thermal-comparison">[jet:thermal-comparison]</a>, $\varepsilon_j\le1$; therefore $$P^b(U_i,U_i)\le P(U_i,U_i)
 \le4 C_\nu\frac K{s_j}(d_{s_j}+|w_i|)m_i.$$ On the other hand, $$D_tU_i\ge
 L\frac{d_{s_j}}{s_j}
       (1+\tfrac78|w_i|^2/d_{s_j}^2)m_i .$$ The ratio $(1+r)/(1+\tfrac78r^2)$ is bounded on $r\ge0$. Choose $L\ge1$ at least $4C_\nu K$ times that bound. Then $D_tU_i\ge P^b(U_i,U_i)$. This choice is made before decreasing $d_0$, so <a href="#jet:thermal-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:thermal-comparison">[jet:thermal-comparison]</a> is compatible with all earlier constraints.

The cold equation implies $D_tc_i\le P^b(c_i,c_i)$, because its loss is nonnegative. To justify comparison explicitly, integrate both inequalities along a free path, using $c_i(0)\le U_i(0)$, and put $z_i=(c_i-U_i)_+$. Positivity and bilinearity give $$z_i(t)\le\int_0^t
  \bigl[2P^b(U_i,z_i)+P^b(z_i,z_i)\bigr]
       (\tau,x-(t-\tau)v,v)\,\,\mathrm d\tau .$$ On any interval of bounded weighted norm, the gain bound Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a> implies $$\|z_i(t)/H\|_\infty
 \le C_I\int_0^t\|z_i(\tau)/H\|_\infty\,\,\mathrm d\tau .$$ Indeed $m_i/H$ is bounded for a fixed scale, uniformly on the preliminary interval, and $z_i\le c_i+U_i$ supplies the other bounded factor. Iteration of this integral inequality, or its scalar integrating factor, gives $z_i=0$. This proves <a href="#jet:cold-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:cold-remainder">[jet:cold-remainder]</a> without a hot bound. The argument is unchanged for each periodic-copy label.

*Summation of escaped tails.* For a nonmain label, <a href="#jet:enlargements" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:enlargements">[jet:enlargements]</a> and $|u_i|\le1+\alpha<2$ imply either $D_{y,i}\ge\delta_j$ or $D_{z,i}\ge b_{\mathrm{sp}}$. Since $\delta_j\le b_{\mathrm{sp}}$, in either case $$D_{y,i}^2+D_{z,i}^2\ge
       (c_\eta/4)^2s_j^4.$$ If $a_v=(2-t)/d_s^2$, then $a_v\ge8$, and completing the velocity square gives $$\sup_v \exp\{4|v|^2-a_v|v-ue_3|^2\}
   =\exp\!\left(\frac{4a_v|u|^2}{a_v-4}\right)
   \le e^{8(1+\alpha)^2}.$$ Thus $c_i\le2m_i$ bounds its escaped contribution by <a id="jet:tail-prefactor"></a>$$C s_j^{-31}e^{-4|v|^2}
 \exp\!\left[-\frac{c_{\mathrm{sp}}
                     (D_{y,i}^2+D_{z,i}^2)}{d_{s_j}^2t}\right].
 \label{jet:tail-prefactor}$$ The factor $d_0^{-3}$ has been included in the fixed constant.

For completeness the integer-copy sum is uniform in the scale. Since $E_j^\sigma\subset[-s_0,s_0]^2$, $$D_{y,i}^2\ge
   \sum_{k=1}^2(|y_k-n_k|-s_0)_+^2,\qquad
 D_{z,i}\ge
   (|z-n_3|-a-2\overline T)_+.$$ For $\beta>0,R\ge0,\epsilon>0$, comparison of the two monotone Gaussian tails with their integrals gives $$\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb R}\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}
 e^{-\beta(|\xi-n|-R)_+^2/\epsilon^2}
 \le 2R+5+\epsilon\sqrt{\pi/\beta}.$$ There are at most $2R+2$ integers in the central interval; for each of the remaining tails, keep its first term and bound the rest by the corresponding Gaussian integral, which proves the displayed bound. Applying this estimate to the three coordinates, with $\epsilon=d_s\sqrt t$, bounds the lattice sum of one half of the spatial exponent uniformly for $t\le\overline T$. Use the other half to retain the escaped distance. For some fixed $\beta_0>0$ this gives $$\sum_{n:\,i\ {\rm nonmain}}
 e^{-c_{\mathrm{sp}}(D_{y,i}^2+D_{z,i}^2)/(d_s^2t)}
 \le C\exp[-\beta_0/(s^{16}t)] .$$ Here $\beta_0$ includes $d_0^{-2}$, and the two signs only contribute a factor two.

For arbitrary $A\ge0$ and $B>0$, choose $p\ge A$ with $16p-31\ge B$. From $e^{-x}\le(p/e)^p x^{-p}$, and $t\le1$, $$s^{-31}e^{-\beta_0/(s^{16}t)}
       \le C_{A,B}t^As^B .$$ The geometric sum of $s_j^B$ is finite. More precisely, restricting to $s_j\le\rho$ gives a bound $C_{A,B}t^A\rho^B$. This proves <a href="#jet:escaped" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:escaped">[jet:escaped]</a>; it also shows that any fixed extra power of $s_j^{-1}$ can be absorbed by increasing $p$. All these estimates were obtained by summing upper bounds over every scale and copy, so they are uniform in $N$.

<a id="eq:core-jacobian"></a>
<a id="jet:density-coefficient"></a>
*The exact free-core velocity measure.* Fix $t,x$ and one label, abbreviating $z_c=z-n_3$, $y_c=y-\bar n$, $s=s_j$, $d=d_s$. For $w\in\mathbb R^3$ define $\Phi(w_3)$ by $$\Phi(w_3)=a_s^\sigma(z_c-t\Phi(w_3))+dw_3,\qquad
 \bar p=d\bar w,\qquad p_3=\Phi(w_3).$$ The same strictly increasing map used in <a href="#jet:scalar-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:scalar-flow">[jet:scalar-flow]</a> gives a unique $\Phi$, and $$\Phi'(w_3)=
 \frac d{1+t\alpha\gamma
           S(\gamma(z_c-t\Phi(w_3))/s)/s}.$$ The full velocity change of variables therefore gives exactly $$\begin{aligned}
 f_i(t,x,p)\,\,\mathrm dp
 ={}&
 \frac{K S(\gamma(z_c-tp_3)/s)}
      {s+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z_c-tp_3)/s)}
 J(w)\\[-2pt]
 &{}\times
 \mathbf 1_{E_j^\sigma}(y_c-td\bar w)
 \mathbf 1_{(-a,a)}(z_c-tp_3)\,\,\mathrm dw .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:core-jacobian}$$ For $k_0=\alpha\gamma>0$ and $0<S\le1$, $$\frac{KS}{s+t k_0S}
 \le\frac K{s+t k_0}
 \le\frac{K\max(1,k_0^{-1})}{s+t}.
 \label{jet:density-coefficient}$$ Thus this estimate is uniform even when $t/s$ is large or the value of $S$ is small.

We spell out the product-sheet upper bound, since the axial law can be very compressed. Let $$g_2(\bar w)=\pi^{-1}e^{-|\bar w|^2},\qquad
 g_1(w_3)=\pi^{-1/2}e^{-w_3^2}.$$ There is a fixed $C_J$ such that $J(w)\le C_Jg_2(\bar w)g_1(w_3)$. The axial map $\Phi$ depends only on $w_3$, and $$|\Phi(w_3)-u_s^\sigma(t,z_c)|\le d|w_3|$$ by the derivative-at-least-one argument. The pushforward of $g_2(\bar w)\,\mathrm d\bar w$ by $\bar w\mapsto d\bar w$ is a centered transverse Gaussian. Independently, let $\rho$ be the pushforward of $g_1(w_3)\,\mathrm dw_3$ by $\Phi$. It is a probability law and, uniformly in $s,t,x$, $$\int_\mathbb Re^{8p_3^2}\,\rho(\,\mathrm dp_3)
 \le e^{16(1+\alpha)^2}
       \int_\mathbb Re^{16d^2w_3^2}g_1(w_3)\,\,\mathrm dw_3
 \le C.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:core-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-jacobian">[eq:core-jacobian]</a> and <a href="#jet:density-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:density-coefficient">[jet:density-coefficient]</a>, after discarding the masks, bound the core measure by $C/(t+s)$ times this product law. No density bound on $\rho$ is asserted or needed.

For the remainder, use $p=u_i e_3+d_s w$ in <a href="#jet:majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:majorant">[jet:majorant]</a>. Its measure is bounded by $$\varepsilon_jm_i\,\,\mathrm dp
 \le C\frac{d_s}{s^2}e^{-(2-t)|w|^2}\,\,\mathrm dw,$$ which is again a product-sheet majorant, with the stated smaller coefficient. Since $$(d_s/s^2)(t+s)=d_0s^8(t+s)
       \le d_0\overline s^8(\overline T+\overline s),$$ this coefficient is also at most $C/(t+s)$. The width restrictions in <a href="#jet:thermal-preliminary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:thermal-preliminary">[jet:thermal-preliminary]</a> allow these Gaussian laws and their fixed, slightly wider majorants in Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a>. Lemma <a href="#ker:parameter-sheets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ker:parameter-sheets">8</a> applies with the common axial bound $A=1+\alpha$, the fixed Gaussian majorant constant, and the displayed physical widths. The factors $C/(t+s)$ and $Cd_s/s^2$ remain outside the probability-sheet normalization. A parameter restriction $|w|>R$ therefore contributes at most $Ce^{-cR^2}$ times its respective amplitude to the normalized gain. For the remainder, $2-t\ge2-\overline T>1$, so its parameter Gaussian is bounded by $e^{-|w|^2}$. The normalized kernel-tail conclusions of Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-tightness">7</a> also apply, with these amplitudes.

The core velocities are supported in $|p|<2$. The remainder has uniformly small Gaussian width and a center in $|p_3|\le1+\alpha$. Substitution in its Gaussian integral gives all its polynomial moments and the uniform Gaussian moment tails. Combined with <a href="#jet:density-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:density-coefficient">[jet:density-coefficient]</a>, these give <a href="#jet:moment-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:moment-envelope">[jet:moment-envelope]</a>.

*The mixed source.* At a fixed point, separate the main color from $e$. Positivity and the ordered nature of the cross-color sum give $$\sum_{i\ne j}P^b(c_i,c_j)
       \le 2P(c_{\mathrm{main}},e)+P(e,e).$$ The function $e^{-4|v|^2}/H(v)$ is essentially bounded, so <a href="#jet:escaped" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:escaped">[jet:escaped]</a> bounds $e$ by $C_A t^AH$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> and the product measures just proved bound $P(c_{\mathrm{main}},H)$ by $C(t+s)^{-1}H$; when there is no main color this term is zero. Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a> bounds $P(H,H)$ by $CH$. Since $t+s\ge t$ and $t\le1$, $$2P(c_{\mathrm{main}},e)+P(e,e)
 \le C_A\{t^A/(t+s)+t^{2A}\}H
 \le C_A' t^{A-1}H .$$ This proves <a href="#jet:mixed-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:mixed-source">[jet:mixed-source]</a>. ◻

</details>

It is useful to record the spatial form of the moment bound. For a fixed $k\ge0$, Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a> implies <a id="jet:spatial-moment-envelope"></a>$$\int_{\mathbb R^3}\langle v\rangle^k c(t,x,v)\,\,\mathrm dv
 \le C_k\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_N}
       \frac{\mathbf 1_{\mathcal O_i(t)}(x)}{t+s_i}
       +C_{A,k}t^A.
 \label{jet:spatial-moment-envelope}$$ At most one term of the displayed sum is present at any point. In one periodic cell, the spatial volume of the enlargement belonging to a fixed annular sign, with its periodic copies included, is at most $Cs_i^2$. The same bound with a factor tending to zero as the velocity cutoff increases holds for the integrated velocity tails.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Quantitative survival on a free core

The upper estimates did not use a hot bound. The following consequence, used only for the dilation, adds the cap assumption.

<a id="stmt-jet-survival"></a>
<div id="jet:survival" class="corollary">

<a id="jet:survival-sandwich"></a>
<a id="jet:stripe-displacement"></a>
<a id="jet:survival-measure"></a>
**Corollary 15** (Survival of a cold core under the cap). *Suppose a finite approximation satisfies $$0\le h(t,x,v)\le(t+s_N)H(v)$$ on an initial interval $[0,T]\subset[0,\overline T]$. All conclusions below concern times $t\in[0,T]$. For every $M\ge0$ there are constants $C,C_M$, independent of $T,N$, the cutoff, and the seed, such that $$\begin{gathered}
 e^{-E_{s,N}(t)}f_i(t,x,v)
       \le c_i(t,x,v)
       \le f_i(t,x,v)+L(d_s/s)m_i(t,x,v),\\
 E_{s,N}(t)=C(d_st/s+t^2+s_Nt)+C_Mt^{M+1}
\end{gathered}
 \label{jet:survival-sandwich}$$ for every label of scale $s$. Consequently, if $r\to0$, $s_N\le r$, $s/r\in[\epsilon,R]$ for fixed $0<\epsilon<R<\infty$, and $0\le t\le\min(T,Ar)$, then $$E_{s,N}(t)\le
 C_{\epsilon,R,A}(d_s+r^2)+C_{M,A}r^{M+1}\longrightarrow0$$ uniformly in the label and in points of its free core. The transverse free displacement divided by the stripe period satisfies $$\frac{td_s}{s^2}\le
       C_{\epsilon,R,A}r^9\longrightarrow0.
 \label{jet:stripe-displacement}$$ On these scales the difference between cold and free-core velocity measures also satisfies, uniformly in position and time, $$r\int_{\mathbb R^3}|c_i-f_i|(t,x,v)\,\,\mathrm dv\longrightarrow0.
 \label{jet:survival-measure}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-survival"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-survival</summary>

*Proof.* The loss-integrating-factor formula for the $i$-th mild equation and nonnegativity of its gain give $$c_i(t,x,v)\ge f_i(t,x,v)
 \exp\left[-\int_0^t
   \nu_F^b(\tau,x-(t-\tau)v,v)\,\,\mathrm d\tau\right].$$ Only points with $f_i(t,x,v)>0$ need be considered. Their entire backward flight, equivalently the corresponding forward flight from the initial support, is in $\mathcal O_i(\tau)$ by Lemma <a href="#jet:barrier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:barrier">13</a>. Also $|v-u_i(\tau,z(\tau))e_3|\le d_s$ throughout it.

At such a position the free part of the same color has velocities within $d_s$ of this center, by <a href="#eq:core-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-jacobian">[eq:core-jacobian]</a>, and mass at most $C/(\tau+s)$. Its contribution to the frequency at $v$ is at most $Cd_s/(\tau+s)\le Cd_s/s$. The remainder has mass $Cd_s/s^2$ and first moment about the center at most $Cd_s^2/s^2$. Since $d_s/s\le1/L$, its contribution is at most $Cd_s/s$ as well. The same-color frequency is therefore bounded by [(jet:escaped)](#jet:escaped)$Cd_s/s$.

Every other color is escaped along this flight. Its total frequency is at most $C_M\tau^M$, by <a href="#jet:escaped" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:escaped">[jet:escaped]</a> and $|v|<2$. The cap applies at every preceding time $\tau\in[0,t]$, so $$\nu_h(v)\le 2\pi(\tau+s_N)
       \int_{\mathbb R^3}(|v|+|p|)H(p)\,\,\mathrm dp
       \le C(\tau+s_N)$$ at these bounded velocities. Since $\nu_F^b\le\nu_F$, integration proves the optical-depth bound in <a href="#jet:survival-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:survival-sandwich">[jet:survival-sandwich]</a>. Its upper bound is <a href="#jet:cold-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:cold-remainder">[jet:cold-remainder]</a>.

If $s/r\in[\epsilon,R]$, $t\le Ar$, and $s_N\le r$, the first term in the optical depth is bounded by $C_{A,\epsilon}d_s$, and the two hot terms are $O_A(r^2)$. All stated convergence is uniform. Moreover $$td_s/s^2=d_0t s^8\le d_0 A R^8r^9,$$ which proves <a href="#jet:stripe-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:stripe-displacement">[jet:stripe-displacement]</a>.

For the last claim the sandwich gives $$|c_i-f_i|\le
       (1-e^{-E_{s,N}(t)})f_i+L(d_s/s)m_i.$$ Integrating in velocity and using the core and remainder density bounds yields $$r\int|c_i-f_i|\,\,\mathrm dv
 \le C E_{s,N}(t)\frac r{t+s}
       +C\frac{r d_s}{s^2}.$$ Here $r/(t+s)\le1/\epsilon$, and $r d_s/s^2=d_0r s^8\le C_Rr^9$. Both terms tend to zero uniformly, proving <a href="#jet:survival-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:survival-measure">[jet:survival-measure]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cap"></a>
### Finite approximations and cap selection

Retain the first $N$ annuli of the datum in <a href="#eq:datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:datum">[eq:datum]</a>, and use a common collision cutoff $b_N\to\infty$ in <a href="#eq:colored" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:colored">[eq:colored]</a>. All assertions in this section first concern fixed $N,b_N$. Constants in the cold estimates and in the caps will be uniform in $N$.

Choose once and for all a nonnegative smooth function $\chi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^3_x\times\mathbb R^3_v)$, not identically zero. After reducing $s_0$, its periodic copies at spatial scale $s_N$ are disjoint initially. The hot initial density is $$h(0,x,v)=\lambda s_N\chi(x/s_N,v),$$ periodized in $x$. The cold initial densities are independent of $\lambda$. In particular the norm $\|\chi(x/s_N,v)\|_H$ is a fixed positive number independent of $N$.

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-cap"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-cap" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16**. *There is a fixed $T_0>0$ such that the following statements hold.*

1.  *With $\lambda=0$, the finite approximations exist on $[0,T_0]$ and satisfy $0\le h\le t^2H$.*

<a id="eq:cap"></a>
2.  *There is a fixed finite $L_0$ such that, for every $0<T\le T_0$ and every $N$, some $\lambda\in[0,L_0]$ gives an approximation on $[0,T]$ with $$\label{eq:cap}
    0\le h(t,x,v)\le(t+s_N)H(v),\qquad
    \max_{0\le t\le T}\frac{\|h(t)\|_H}{t+s_N}=1.$$*

*In both cases all cold components obey Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-finite-cap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-finite-cap</summary>

*Proof.* We first give the fixed-approximation local theory used in the selection. Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a> bounds the gain bilinearly in $\|\cdot\|_H$. For the cutoff loss, $$\|\nu_f^{b_N}\|_\infty
 \le2\pi b_N\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_x\int_{\mathbb R^3}|f(x,w)|\,\mathrm dw
 \le2\pi b_N\|f\|_H\int_{\mathbb R^3}H(w)\,\mathrm dw,$$ so gain and loss are locally Lipschitz on bounded balls of the weighted norm. Free transport is an isometry in that norm.

Picard iteration for the integral equation along characteristics gives a unique local solution in the space of bounded measurable functions with finite weighted norm. An integrating factor for the loss, followed by positive iteration of the gain, gives nonnegativity. One can equivalently perform this iteration for the labeled system, using the norm of the sum of the absolute values of all components, and for differences the sum of the absolute differences. The quadratic estimates close in that norm. The Gaussian spatial tails in Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a> make the sums over periodic copies finite in the relevant norm. Periodic covariance is preserved by uniqueness.

The solution continues as long as its total weighted norm remains bounded. It depends continuously on its initial datum, uniformly on a fixed interval on which that norm is bounded. These assertions follow from the same integral contraction and its difference estimate on short subintervals. They do not require norm continuity of spatial translations: one works with the integral equation along the free paths. In particular, $$\|h(t)-h(s,x-(t-s)v,v)\|_H\le C_{N,b_N}|t-s|$$ on a bounded solution interval. Since transport is an isometry, $t\mapsto\|h(t)\|_H$, and hence $t\mapsto\|h(t)\|_H/(t+s_N)$, are continuous.

<a id="cap:scalar"></a>
Let $z(t)=\|h(t)\|_H$. Dropping the loss in the hot equation, the cold and kernel estimates give its scalar integral comparison with $$\label{cap:scalar}
z'(t)\le
 \left(\frac{C_1}{t+s_N}+C_1\right)z(t)+C_1z(t)^2+C_A t^{A-1}.$$ Here $A$ can be any sufficiently large fixed number, and its source constant is uniform in $N$. This is an upper integral inequality; no differentiability of the essential supremum is needed.

Fix $A>C_1+2$. Choose $D>0$ so that $(A-C_1)D>C_A$. Since $t/(t+s_N)\le1$, the function $Dt^A$ is a supersolution of <a href="#cap:scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cap:scalar">[cap:scalar]</a> after shortening a fixed preliminary time interval to absorb the bounded linear and quadratic terms. Shorten it again to ensure $Dt^A\le t^2$. Comparison from zero proves (a). The cold bound is independent of $h$, so it also ensures continuation for each fixed $N,b_N$ up to this time.

For (b), shorten $T_0$ to be less than one, so that the zero-seed solution satisfies a strict cap: $$\frac{\|h(t)\|_H}{t+s_N}\le\frac{t^2}{t+s_N}\le T_0<1.$$ Choose $L_0$ so large that $L_0\|\chi(x/s_N,v)\|_H>1$. The cap then fails at time zero for $\lambda=L_0$.

For fixed $N,b_N,T$, let $\mathcal O$ be the set of seed parameters in $[0,L_0]$ whose solution exists up to $T$ and obeys a strict cap there. Continuous dependence and continuation make $\mathcal O$ relatively open. Its component containing zero has a right endpoint $\lambda_*$ strictly below $L_0$: indeed a neighborhood of $L_0$ already fails the initial cap. Approach $\lambda_*$ from within that component. The cap and the fixed cold bound give a common finite weighted norm on $[0,T]$, with constants permitted to depend on $N,b_N$. The local difference estimates on finitely many short subintervals give a limit solution at $\lambda_*$, with cap at most one. It continues to $T$. If its maximum cap ratio were less than one, continuous dependence would extend the component past $\lambda_*$, a contradiction. This proves <a href="#eq:cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cap">[eq:cap]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cap-parameter-order"></a>
<div id="cap:parameter-order" class="remark">

*Remark 17*. The coefficient $C_1$ may be large, in particular when $\alpha\gamma$ is small. It is fixed before $A,D,T_0$ are chosen. The arbitrary-power tail estimates are available on a preliminary short interval; their constants can be fixed there before the final shortening. Thus the choice does not depend on $N$, and imposes no restriction on the later choice of shrinking horizons.

</div>

<a id="sec:admissibility"></a>
### Fixed-horizon limits and admissibility

In this section the physical horizon $T\in(0,T_0]$ is fixed. The approximation index tends to infinity without any rescaling of time or space. This distinction matters: the cold densities in this section have uniformly integrable limits, whereas their rescalings in the next section will converge to measures in velocity.

<a id="stmt-prop-admissible-limits"></a>
<div id="prop:admissible-limits" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18**. *Fix $T\in(0,T_0]$. Either the zero-seed approximations of Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-cap">16</a>, or any sequence of its cap shots on $[0,T]$, has a subsequence whose total densities $F_N=c_N+h_N$ converge weakly in $L^1([0,T]\times\Omega)$ to a nonnegative density $F$. The subsequence can be chosen so that $F_N(t)\rightharpoonup F(t)$ in $L^1(\Omega)$ at every $t\in[0,T]$, with uniformly small polynomially weighted velocity tails. The limit has the common initial datum <a href="#eq:datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:datum">[eq:datum]</a>, and $$Q^\pm(F,F)\in L^1([0,T]\times\Omega),\qquad
F\in C([0,T];L^1(\Omega)).$$ It satisfies every condition of Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">2</a> on this interval, including the inequalities at every time. Each such limit has an admissible global continuation. The dormant upper bound, consisting of the cold majorants of Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a> and $t^2H$, passes to a zero-seed limit.*

</div>

We give the compactness and entropy arguments in detail.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Conservation and entropy before passage to the limit

At a fixed approximation the bounds already proved imply $F_N\leq A_N H$ on the whole solution interval, with a finite constant allowed to depend on $N,b_N,T$. In particular there are all polynomial velocity moments, and there is an $L^p$ majorant for some $1<p<2/q$. The equation holds absolutely continuously on almost every free path. Its cutoff gain and loss are integrable, including after multiplication by any fixed polynomial in velocity.

The collision map preserves volume, kinetic energy, and $|v-v_*|$, so it also preserves the cutoff. For an integrable velocity test $\psi$ the collision symmetry gives $$\int_{\mathbb R^3}\psi(v)Q^{b_N}(F_N,F_N)(v)\,\mathrm dv
=\frac12\int_{\mathbb R^3\times\mathbb R^3\times S^2}
 |a|\mathbf 1_{\{|v-v_*|<b_N\}}F_NF_{N,*}
 (\psi'+\psi_*'-\psi-\psi_*)\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dv_*\,\mathrm d\omega.$$ The right side vanishes for $\psi=1,v_j,|v|^2$. Integrating the path equation and using periodicity therefore proves mass, momentum, and energy conservation at every time of the finite approximation. The same calculation with a spatially dependent mass test gives its local continuity equation.

<a id="comp:log-bound"></a>
To justify entropy at zeros, put $E(v)=e^{-2|v|^2}$, take $0<\delta\leq1$, and set $F_{N,\delta}=F_N+\delta E$. Define $$\mathcal A_\delta(f,v)
=M(v)+\int_0^f\log\frac{u+\delta E(v)}{M(v)}\,\mathrm du.$$ Its derivative with respect to $f$ is $\log(F_{N,\delta}/M)$. At the fixed approximation, $$\label{comp:log-bound}
\left|\log\frac{F_{N,\delta}}M\right|
\leq C_N\bigl(1+|\log\delta|+|v|^2
                   +\log^+|\bar v|^{-1}\bigr).$$ Indeed $F_{N,\delta}\geq\delta e^{-2|v|^2}$ gives the lower logarithmic bound, and $F_N\leq A_NH$ gives the upper bound. Every factor on the right of <a href="#comp:log-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:log-bound">[comp:log-bound]</a> is integrable against $H$, apart from the harmless constant $|\log\delta|$: in the transverse plane this uses $\int_0^1 r^{1-q}|\log r|\,\mathrm dr<\infty$.

<a id="comp:regularized-entropy"></a>
Since $E\leq C H$, the fixed-cutoff bilinear bounds imply $$|Q^{b_N}(F_{N,\delta},F_{N,\delta})
            -Q^{b_N}(F_N,F_N)|\leq C_{N,b_N}\delta H.$$ Consequently replacing the latter operator by the former in its integral against $\log(F_{N,\delta}/M)$ costs at most $C_{N,b_N,T}\delta(1+|\log\delta|)$. The path chain rule, followed by spatial integration, now gives $$\label{comp:regularized-entropy}
\int_\Omega\mathcal A_\delta(F_N(t),v)
 +\int_0^t\mathcal D_{b_N}(F_{N,\delta}(s))\,\mathrm ds
\leq\int_\Omega\mathcal A_\delta(F_N(0),v)
       +C_{N,b_N,T}\delta(1+|\log\delta|).$$ Here $\mathcal D_b$ denotes the dissipation with the invariant cutoff inserted. The collision identity used in this step is $$\int_\Omega Q^{b_N}(F_{N,\delta},F_{N,\delta})
       \log(F_{N,\delta}/M)
=-\mathcal D_{b_N}(F_{N,\delta}).$$ It follows by averaging over interchange of the two particles and over the elastic substitution. The $\log M$ term cancels because it is a linear combination of mass and energy. All integrals for this calculation are finite at $\delta>0$ by <a href="#comp:log-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:log-bound">[comp:log-bound]</a>.

<a id="comp:finite-entropy"></a>
As $\delta\downarrow0$, the primitive converges to $f\log(f/M)-f+M$. Its integrals converge as well. For example its lower bound is that limiting primitive, while its upper bound follows by replacing $\delta$ by one in the increasing integrand. The negative part of $f\log f$ is integrable under a finite second moment, and the upper bound is integrable by <a href="#comp:log-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:log-bound">[comp:log-bound]</a> without the $|\log\delta|$ term. Fatou’s lemma applied to the nonnegative dissipation integrand in <a href="#comp:regularized-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:regularized-entropy">[comp:regularized-entropy]</a> therefore yields, for every $t\in[0,T]$, $$\label{comp:finite-entropy}
\mathcal H_M(F_N(t))+\int_0^t\mathcal D_{b_N}(F_N(s))\,\mathrm ds
\leq\mathcal H_M(F_N(0)).$$

Lemma <a href="#lem:datum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:datum">12</a> gives convergence of the initial mass, energy, and relative entropy to those of <a href="#eq:datum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:datum">[eq:datum]</a>. Its estimates include the hot seed, whose mass and energy are $O(s_N^4)$ and whose absolute entropy is $O(s_N^4(1+|\log s_N|))$, as well as the seed’s overlap with the cold density in the relative entropy. The convergence is uniform for the allowed seed parameters $0\leq\lambda\leq L_0$.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Uniform integrability of densities and collision products

For $s=s_j$, let $A_s$ be the annulus $\{y\in\mathbb R^2:s<|y|<e^\eta s\}$, viewed as a subset of $\mathbb T^2$. By Lemma <a href="#jet:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:geometry">11</a>, these annuli are pairwise disjoint and contain the enlarged stripes of both signs at their respective scales. Their areas satisfy $|A_s|\leq Cs^2$. Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a>, the cap, and the dormant bound imply, for every fixed $k\geq0$, <a id="comp:moment-envelope"></a>$$\label{comp:moment-envelope}
R_{k,N}(t,x):=\int_{\mathbb R^3}\langle v\rangle^kF_N(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv
\leq C_k\left(1+\sum_{j\leq N}
                  \frac{\mathbf 1_{A_{s_j}}(y)}{t+s_j}\right).$$ The bounded term includes the hot density and escaped tails. The cold remainder has density coefficient $O(d_s/s^2)=O(s^8)$, and is also covered by this estimate. All constants are independent of $N$.

<a id="comp:annular-square"></a>
<a id="comp:small-annuli"></a>
The square of the envelope is integrable uniformly in $N$, including at the initial surface. In fact the contribution of one annulus is $$\label{comp:annular-square}
\int_0^T\frac{Cs^2}{(t+s)^2}\,\mathrm dt
=Cs^2\left(\frac1s-\frac1{T+s}\right)\leq Cs.$$ Since the scales form a geometric sequence, $\sum_{s_j<\varepsilon}s_j\leq C_\eta\varepsilon$. If $A_{<\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{s_j<\varepsilon}A_{s_j}$, disjointness of the annuli thus gives $$\label{comp:small-annuli}
\sup_N\int_0^T\int_{A_{<\varepsilon}\times\mathbb T}
                 R_{k,N}(t,x)^2\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dt\longrightarrow0
\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow0).$$ The contribution of the bounded term is at most $C T|A_{<\varepsilon}|$.

<a id="comp:fixed-majorant"></a>
Outside $A_{<\varepsilon}$, the main color has one of finitely many scales $s_j\geq\varepsilon$. Those velocity widths are positive and fixed. Their Gaussian bounds, with axial centers in a fixed bounded interval, give a function $\Psi_\varepsilon(v)$, independent of $N,t,x$, such that $$\label{comp:fixed-majorant}
F_N(t,x,v)\leq\Psi_\varepsilon(v)
\quad\hbox{if }y\notin A_{<\varepsilon},\qquad
\int\langle v\rangle^k\Psi_\varepsilon(v)\,\mathrm dv<\infty
\quad(k\geq0).$$ One may take a finite sum of broadened Gaussians about the bounded axial interval, together with a multiple of $H$ for the hot component and escaped tails. There is no need for this majorant to be bounded on the velocity axis.

<a id="comp:fixed-time-small"></a>
Equations <a href="#comp:small-annuli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:small-annuli">[comp:small-annuli]</a>–<a href="#comp:fixed-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-majorant">[comp:fixed-majorant]</a> prove uniform integrability of $$\langle v\rangle^k\langle w\rangle^k
 F_N(t,x,v)F_N(t,x,w)$$ in the full spacetime and two-velocity variables. Indeed its integral over the removed annuli is at most the left side of <a href="#comp:small-annuli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:small-annuli">[comp:small-annuli]</a>; elsewhere it has the fixed integrable majorant $\langle v\rangle^k\Psi_\varepsilon(v)
  \langle w\rangle^k\Psi_\varepsilon(w)$. This also proves uniformly small velocity tails. Taking first powers instead proves uniform integrability of the one-particle densities in spacetime. At individual times the estimate is $$\label{comp:fixed-time-small}
\sup_{N,\,0\leq t\leq T}
 \int_{A_{<\varepsilon}\times\mathbb T}R_{k,N}(t,x)\,\mathrm dx
\leq C_k\sum_{s_j<C\varepsilon}\frac{s_j^2}{s_j}
       +C_k|A_{<\varepsilon}|\longrightarrow0.$$ Together with <a href="#comp:fixed-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-majorant">[comp:fixed-majorant]</a>, this proves weighted one-particle uniform integrability and velocity tightness uniformly at all individual times.

The gains and losses enjoy the same spacetime conclusion. To see this directly, collision symmetry and energy conservation imply $$\int\langle v\rangle^k
 Q_N^\pm(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv\leq C_k R_{k+1,N}(t,x)^2,
\qquad Q_N^\pm=Q^{\pm,b_N}(F_N,F_N).$$ Their weighted integrals on the small annuli therefore tend uniformly to zero. On the complement, positivity and <a href="#comp:fixed-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-majorant">[comp:fixed-majorant]</a> give the fixed bounds $$Q_N^+(t,x,v)\leq P(\Psi_\varepsilon,\Psi_\varepsilon)(v),\qquad
Q_N^-(t,x,v)\leq\Psi_\varepsilon(v)\nu_{\Psi_\varepsilon}(v).$$ Both right sides are integrable with every fixed polynomial velocity weight: integrate first in the output velocity and use the preceding collision-moment inequality for the fixed function $\Psi_\varepsilon$. Thus $\{Q_N^\pm\}$ is uniformly integrable in spacetime, with uniformly small weighted velocity tails. In particular its integrals over time intervals of length tending to zero vanish uniformly in $N$. This last assertion follows by first discarding the small annuli and then integrating the fixed majorants over the time interval.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### An elementary averaging argument

We use a uniformly integrable version of velocity averaging (Golse et al. 1988, Proposition 3) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GolseLionsPerthameSentis1988](#ref-GolseLionsPerthameSentis1988)]. The following proof gives the precise local statement needed here.

<a id="stmt-comp-averaging"></a>
<div id="comp:averaging" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19**. *Suppose that $f_n$ and $g_n$ are locally uniformly integrable in $L^1$ on an open time-space-velocity region, and $(\partial_t+v\cdot\nabla_x)f_n=g_n$ there in distributions. Then for every smooth compactly supported velocity test $\psi$, the averages $\int\psi(v)f_n(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv$ are relatively compact in local $L^1_{t,x}$ on regions where the hypotheses hold for velocities in a neighborhood of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi$. The assertion applies both on a spatial torus and locally in $\mathbb R^3$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-averaging"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-averaging</summary>

*Proof.* Choose smooth interior cutoffs in time, space, and velocity; the spatial cutoff is unnecessary on a torus. Write the localized sequence as $q_n$, extend it by zero outside the localization interval, and put $r_n=(1+\partial_t+v\cdot\nabla_x)q_n$. The equation shows that $r_n$ is uniformly integrable in $L^1$, with support in a fixed bounded set of time and velocity and, in the Euclidean case, space. The derivative of a spatial cutoff is harmless because velocity has also been localized. The exact identity $$q_n(t,x,v)=\int_0^\infty e^{-s}r_n(t-s,x-sv,v)\,\mathrm ds$$ follows by integrating the localized equation from a time preceding its support. Denote this damped inverse by $R$. It is an $L^1$ contraction, since free transport preserves Lebesgue measure.

Truncate $r_n$ at height $L$, retaining its sign, to obtain $r_n^{(L)}$. Uniform integrability gives $$\sup_n\|r_n-r_n^{(L)}\|_1\longrightarrow0,
\qquad
\sup_n\|r_n^{(L)}\|_2<\infty
\quad\hbox{for fixed }L.$$ The averages of $R(r_n-r_n^{(L)})$ are therefore uniformly small in $L^1$. It remains to handle a bounded $L^2$ family $r_n^{(L)}$.

Take Fourier transform in $(t,x)$, using Fourier series in the periodic coordinates. For $u_n=Rr_n^{(L)}$, Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\left|\int\psi(v)\widehat u_n(\tau,k,v)\,\mathrm dv\right|^2
\leq I_\psi(\tau,k)
       \int|\widehat r_n^{(L)}(\tau,k,v)|^2\,\mathrm dv,
\qquad
I_\psi(\tau,k)=\int
 \frac{|\psi(v)|^2}{1+(\tau+k\cdot v)^2}\,\mathrm dv.$$ If $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi\subset\{|v|\leq R_0\}$, integration in the direction of $k$ gives $I_\psi\leq C_\psi/|k|$ when $k\ne0$. If $|\tau|>2R_0|k|$, the denominator instead gives $I_\psi\leq C_\psi/(1+|\tau|^2)$. It follows that $I_\psi(\tau,k)\to0$ uniformly as $|\tau|+|k|\to\infty$. The averages consequently have uniformly small high-frequency $L^2$ tails. Convolution with a fixed smooth approximate identity approximates them uniformly in $L^2$. On each compact time-space set the convolutions are bounded and equicontinuous, by Cauchy–Schwarz applied to translates of the convolution kernel and their derivatives. They are therefore relatively compact there. This proves local $L^2$, hence local $L^1$, compactness for fixed $L$. The uniform $L^1$ error as $L\to\infty$ proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Identification of the nonlinear terms

The uniform integrability just proved supplies a subsequence with $F_N\rightharpoonup F$ in spacetime $L^1$; velocity tightness allows the whole velocity space. Since $D_tF_N=Q_N^+-Q_N^-$, Lemma <a href="#comp:averaging" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:averaging">19</a> applies. After a diagonal extraction for a countable dense family of compact velocity tests, all such velocity averages converge strongly in local $L^1_{t,x}$ to the corresponding averages of $F$. Extension from that family to every smooth compact velocity test uses the uniform mass bound.

<a id="comp:pair-limit"></a>
Set $W_N(t,x,v,w)=F_N(t,x,v)F_N(t,x,w)$. The full pair-product uniform integrability supplies a weak $L^1$ limit $W$, after another extraction. For smooth compact velocity tests $\psi,\chi$, $$\int\psi(v)\chi(w)W_N\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dw
=\left(\int\psi F_N\,\mathrm dv\right)
 \left(\int\chi F_N\,\mathrm dw\right).$$ Both factors converge in local $L^1$, so their product converges in measure. The products are uniformly integrable in $(t,x)$, by <a href="#comp:annular-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:annular-square">[comp:annular-square]</a> and the fixed majorants after removal of small annuli. Their convergence is consequently in $L^1$: to verify this last implication, discard a set of arbitrarily small measure on which convergence is not uniform, and use uniform integrability on the discarded set. We obtain $$\int\psi(v)\chi(w)W\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dw
=\left(\int\psi F\,\mathrm dv\right)
 \left(\int\chi F\,\mathrm dw\right).$$ Finite sums of separated velocity tests are uniformly dense among continuous functions on compact two-velocity sets. Testing also in $(t,x)$ identifies $$\label{comp:pair-limit}
W_N\rightharpoonup F(t,x,v)F(t,x,w)
\quad\hbox{in }L^1([0,T]\times\mathbb T^3\times\mathbb R^3\times\mathbb R^3).$$ The same conclusion holds with any fixed polynomial weight for which it is needed. Interior time compactness suffices: the uniform integrability already proved removes intervals near zero and $T$.

Adjoining the finite angular measure preserves weak convergence. The elastic map on $(v,w,\omega)$ is measure preserving and involutive, so <a href="#comp:pair-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:pair-limit">[comp:pair-limit]</a> also identifies the primed pair product. On a bounded two-velocity set the factor $|a|$ is bounded, and the cutoff is eventually absent. The weighted tail estimates allow these sets to exhaust all velocities. Since $Q_N^\pm$ themselves are uniformly integrable, testing their weak limits and using these identifications proves <a id="comp:collision-limit"></a>$$\label{comp:collision-limit}
Q_N^\pm\rightharpoonup Q^\pm(F,F)
\quad\hbox{in }L^1([0,T]\times\Omega).$$ Passing the equation, including its initial boundary term, is now legitimate. The initial densities converge in $L^1$, and the limit satisfies the unrenormalized distributional equation with datum $F_0$.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Every-time traces, entropy, and the chain rule

We first specify representatives at every time. For a smooth periodic test $\varphi(x,v)$ compactly supported in velocity, the finite path equations give $$|\langle F_N(t)-F_N(s),\varphi\rangle|
\leq\int_s^t\int_\Omega
 \bigl(F_N|v\cdot\nabla_x\varphi|
       +(Q_N^++Q_N^-)|\varphi|\bigr)\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm du.$$ The first term is bounded by $C_\varphi|t-s|$; the second has a uniform modulus tending to zero with $|t-s|$, by the time-integral conclusion above. For a countable uniformly dense collection of such tests, extract uniform convergence of their pairings on $[0,T]$. At each individual time <a href="#comp:fixed-time-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-time-small">[comp:fixed-time-small]</a> and <a href="#comp:fixed-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-majorant">[comp:fixed-majorant]</a> make $\{F_N(t)\}$ relatively weakly compact in $L^1$. Every cluster point has the prescribed smooth pairings, hence is the same density. Thus this single subsequence converges weakly in $L^1$ at every time. It defines a weakly continuous representative against smooth tests, agreeing with the spacetime limit. The weighted fixed-time tightness also passes total mass, momentum, and energy from the finite approximations. In particular energy is conserved on this early interval, although only its inequality will be needed.

<a id="comp:limit-entropy"></a>
Let $\Phi(A,B)=(A-B)\log(A/B)$ with its nonnegative extended values. It is jointly convex and lower semicontinuous: it is the sum of the two convex perspectives $A\log(A/B)$ and $B\log(B/A)$. On every bounded collision set, <a href="#comp:pair-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:pair-limit">[comp:pair-limit]</a> and its primed version therefore give lower semicontinuity of the integral of $|a|\Phi$. One can absorb the weight into the arguments because $\Phi(cA,cB)=c\Phi(A,B)$ for $c\geq0$. The lower semicontinuity follows, for example, by expressing a nonnegative convex lower-semicontinuous function as the supremum of its affine supporting functions and using weak convergence on a finite partition. Exhausting bounded velocity sets, and using that the cutoff is eventually absent on each of them, gives for every $t\in[0,T]$ $$\int_0^t\mathcal D(F(s))\,\mathrm ds
\leq\liminf_N\int_0^t\mathcal D_{b_N}(F_N(s))\,\mathrm ds.$$ The same convex lower-semicontinuity argument applies to $\mathcal H_M(F_N(t))$ under weak $L^1$ convergence at that time. Combining the two nonnegative terms, using <a href="#comp:finite-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:finite-entropy">[comp:finite-entropy]</a> and initial entropy convergence, proves $$\label{comp:limit-entropy}
\mathcal H_M(F(t))+\int_0^t\mathcal D(F(s))\,\mathrm ds
\leq\mathcal H_M(F_0)\qquad(0\leq t\leq T).$$ There is no time-dependent subsequence in this conclusion.

<a id="comp:path-formula"></a>
In fact the time representative is strongly continuous. In free coordinates the distributional equation and its initial trace read $$\partial_t\bigl[F(t,x+tv,v)\bigr]=Q(F,F)(t,x+tv,v).$$ The right side is integrable in $(t,x,v)$. Subtracting its Bochner integral leaves a distribution with zero time derivative, whose initial value is $F_0$. Therefore $$\label{comp:path-formula}
F(t,x+tv,v)=F_0(x,v)
 +\int_0^t Q(F,F)(s,x+sv,v)\,\mathrm ds$$ as an $L^1_{x,v}$ identity, initially for almost every time and then for every time by the representative just constructed. The right side is absolutely continuous in $L^1$. Free transport is a strongly continuous group on $L^1(\mathbb T^3\times\mathbb R^3)$, as follows by approximating an $L^1$ function by continuous functions with compact velocity support. Thus $F\in C([0,T];L^1)$. This proves in particular continuity against every bounded measurable test.

Equation <a href="#comp:path-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:path-formula">[comp:path-formula]</a> also holds on almost every individual free path. Since $M(v)>0$ is constant on such a path, the ordinary absolutely continuous chain rule gives, for every $\beta$ in Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">2</a>, $$D_t\bigl[M\beta(F/M)\bigr]=\beta'(F/M)Q(F,F).$$ The source is integrable because $|\beta'|\leq C_\beta$ and $Q\in L^1$. Moreover $$|M\beta(f/M)-M\beta(g/M)|\leq C_\beta|f-g|,
\qquad |M\beta(f/M)|\leq C_\beta f.$$ Hence the renormalization is strongly continuous at the initial and terminal traces. Integrating its path equation proves exactly the renormalized identity and trace in Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">2</a>. The separate integrability requirement follows from $0\leq Q^\pm/(1+F/M)\leq Q^\pm$.

Integrating the unrenormalized equation in velocity is justified by absolute collision integrability. The collision integral has zero mass, and the flux is integrable, so it gives the required local continuity equation with its initial density. Momentum has already passed at every time. Finally, the negative part of $F\log F$ is bounded uniformly by mass and energy. For example split $\{F<1\}$ at $F=e^{-1-|v|^2}$: above that threshold use $-F\log F\leq(1+|v|^2)F$, and below it use a fixed multiple of $e^{-(1+|v|^2)/2}$. Equation <a href="#comp:limit-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:limit-entropy">[comp:limit-entropy]</a> then bounds the positive part as well. This proves the uniform absolute logarithmic integral required in the definition.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Continuation and inherited bounds

At time $T$, the density just constructed has finite mass, energy, and absolute logarithmic integral. We use the global existence theorem of DiPerna and Lions (DiPerna and Lions 1989, 1991) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[DiPernaLions1989](#ref-DiPernaLions1989), [DiPernaLions1991](#ref-DiPernaLions1991)], in the precise periodic formulation of (Levermore and Masmoudi 2010, sec. 4.1, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[LevermoreMasmoudi2010](#ref-LevermoreMasmoudi2010)].
