We verify its interface with Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">2</a> before concatenating.

For our kernel $b_{\mathrm{phys}}(\omega,v-w)=|(v-w)\cdot\omega|$, local integrability holds and, for every compact $K\subset\mathbb R^3$, $$\frac1{1+|v|^2}\int_{K\times S^2}
 b_{\mathrm{phys}}(\omega,v-w)\,\mathrm d\omega\,\mathrm dw
=\frac{2\pi}{1+|v|^2}\int_K|v-w|\,\mathrm dw\longrightarrow0
\quad(|v|\to\infty).$$ This is the hypothesis of that theorem. To match its collision and time normalizations, put $$C_b=\int_{\mathbb R^3\times\mathbb R^3\times S^2}
 b_{\mathrm{phys}}(\omega,v-w)M(v)M(w)\,\mathrm d\omega\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dw,
\qquad \epsilon=C_b^{-1}.$$ The constant $C_b$ is finite and positive. Use the source kernel $b_{\mathrm{phys}}/C_b$ and its equation $\epsilon\partial_sG+v\cdot\nabla_xG
=\epsilon^{-1}Q_{b_{\mathrm{phys}}/C_b}(G,G)$, with initial density $F(T)/M$. Here the relative-density operator satisfies $M Q_{b_{\mathrm{phys}}/C_b}(G,G)=Q(MG,MG)/C_b$. Then $\widetilde F(t,x,v)=M(v)G(\epsilon t,x,v)$ has precisely our collision normalization. The same time substitution converts the source dissipation coefficient $\epsilon^{-2}$ into the coefficient one of $\mathcal D$.

The cited theorem allows arbitrary finite relative-entropy initial data. It gives weak continuity of $G$ in $L^1(M\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dv)$, which is exactly weak continuity of $\widetilde F$ in $L^1(\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dv)$, and the renormalized identities with the initial trace for continuously differentiable functions satisfying $|\beta'(z)|\le C/\sqrt{1+z}$. Our renormalizations are included. Its local mass identity is the one required here. Integrating its momentum identity over the torus removes the spatial divergence of the momentum defect, giving total momentum conservation. This extends to every time by weak continuity and the uniform second-moment bound: the first-moment tail outside $|v|\le R$ is at most $R^{-1}$ times the second moment. Dropping the nonnegative defect contributions in the theorem’s energy and relative-entropy inequalities gives the two inequalities in Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">2</a> at every time. The negative-entropy estimate above then supplies the uniform absolute logarithmic integral.

We also need separate global integrability of the renormalized gain and loss, rather than only their difference. For any density $f\ge0$, write $g=f/M$. Since $f/(1+g)\le M$, $$\int_\Omega\frac{Q^-(f,f)}{1+g}
\le\int_\Omega M\nu_f
\le C\int_\Omega(1+|v|)f.$$ For $A,B\ge0$, with the same extended-value convention as for the dissipation, $$A\le2B+\frac2{\log2}(A-B)\log(A/B).$$ Indeed the first term suffices when $A\le2B$; otherwise $A-B>A/2$ and $\log(A/B)>\log2$. Apply this with $A=f'f_*'$, $B=ff_*$, divide by $1+g(v)$, and integrate with the collision kernel. Nonnegativity of the dissipation gives $$\int_\Omega\frac{Q^+(f,f)}{1+g}
\le2\int_\Omega\frac{Q^-(f,f)}{1+g}
  +\frac8{\log2}\mathcal D(f).$$ The moment and entropy-dissipation bounds for $\widetilde F$ make both terms integrable over every finite time interval. Thus the continuation satisfies all of Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">2</a>.

Concatenate $\widetilde F$ with $F$ at time $T$. The two weak $L^1$ traces agree, so the concatenation is continuous against every bounded measurable test. Its renormalized identity follows by adding the two identities: the early terminal term $M\beta(F(T)/M)$ cancels the continuation’s prescribed initial term. The Lipschitz estimate above supplies that early terminal trace. The mass continuity identities concatenate in the same way.

For $t\geq T$, add the continuation inequality $$\mathcal H_M(\widetilde F(t-T))
 +\int_0^{t-T}\mathcal D(\widetilde F(s))\,\mathrm ds
\leq\mathcal H_M(F(T))$$ to <a href="#comp:limit-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:limit-entropy">[comp:limit-entropy]</a> at $T$. This gives the global entropy inequality, with no entropy equality at the splice assumed. Energy inequalities and momentum conservation concatenate using the common terminal moments. Collision integrability in the renormalized sense holds on both pieces, hence on every bounded interval of the concatenation. The uniform moment and absolute entropy bounds on such an interval follow from those of the two pieces.

For a dormant sequence, sum the cold upper bounds of Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a> over all labels and add $t^2H$. This is a common, locally integrable pointwise upper bound for the finite total densities. Testing against nonnegative functions and passing to the weak limit preserves it. All assertions of Proposition <a href="#prop:admissible-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:admissible-limits">18</a> are now proved.

<a id="sec:dilation"></a>
### The cap dilation and its linear limit

<a id="eq:rescaling"></a>
<a id="dil:cap-bound"></a>
We now let the physical horizons tend to zero. Choose cap shots with $T_N\downarrow0$, terminal annular scales satisfying $s_N/T_N\to0$, and cutoffs $b_N\to\infty$. Relabeling a subsequence in this way does not require that every integer index occur. Let $t_{*,N}\in[0,T_N]$ be a time attaining the cap. In the periodic lift about the origin define $$\label{eq:rescaling}
\begin{gathered}
r_N=t_{*,N}+s_N,\qquad
h_{r_N}(t,x,v)=r_N^{-1}h_N(r_Nt,r_Nx,v),\qquad
C_{r_N}(t,x,v)=r_Nc_N(r_Nt,r_Nx,v),\\
l_N=\frac{s_N}{r_N}\longrightarrow l\in[0,1],\qquad
U_N=\frac{T_N}{r_N}\longrightarrow U\in[1,\infty].
\end{gathered}$$ The limits are taken after subselection, using the compactification of $[0,\infty)$ for the second ratio. We have $r_N\to0$, and $U\geq1$ follows from $U_N\geq(1+s_N/T_N)^{-1}$. The prelimit inequality $U_N\geq1$ is not needed. The dilated cap event occurs at time $1-l_N$, and $$\label{dil:cap-bound}
0\leq h_{r_N}(t,x,v)\leq(t+l_N)H(v),
\qquad 0\leq t\leq U_N.$$ When convenient we suppress the index on $r_N$.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The limiting mesh and background

The dilated annular scales are $\mu_{N,j}=s_j/r_N$, $j\leq N$. Their ratio is $e^\eta$, their smallest member is $l_N$, and their largest member tends to infinity. Subselect their logarithmic phase modulo $\eta$. The grids then converge on compact subintervals of $(0,\infty)$ to a geometric mesh $\mathcal M$ unbounded above. If $l>0$, its members are $l,e^\eta l,e^{2\eta}l,\ldots$. If $l=0$, it is a two-sided geometric mesh with no smallest member. To verify this description, choose a grid point in $(e^{-\eta},1]$, pass to its limit, and multiply it by the fixed factors $e^{j\eta}$. When the smallest point stays positive its integer distance in the grid from that chosen point is bounded, so can also be fixed by subselection. Coincident phase endpoints give the same mesh.

<a id="eq:background"></a>
For each $\mu>0$ and $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}$, let $u_{\mu,\sigma}(t,z)$ be the unique solution of $$u=\sigma+\alpha\tanh\frac{\gamma(z-tu)}\mu.$$ The left side minus the right side has derivative in $u$ equal to $1+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tu)/\mu)/\mu>0$; its values have opposite signs for sufficiently large positive and negative $u$. This proves existence and uniqueness. The implicit function theorem gives smoothness, and $$|u_{\mu,\sigma}-\sigma|\leq\alpha,
\qquad
\partial_z u_{\mu,\sigma}
=\frac{\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tu)/\mu)}
       {\mu+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tu)/\mu)}\geq0.$$ On an open mesh annulus $\mu<|y|<e^\eta\mu$, define the nonnegative velocity measure $$\label{eq:background}
\begin{split}
B(t,x,\,\mathrm dp)
&=\sum_{\sigma=\pm1}b_{\mu,\sigma}(t,z)
                         \delta_{u_{\mu,\sigma}(t,z)e_3}(\,\mathrm dp),\\
b_{\mu,\sigma}(t,z)
&=\frac{\theta K S(\gamma(z-tu_{\mu,\sigma})/\mu)}
 {\mu+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tu_{\mu,\sigma})/\mu)}.
\end{split}$$ Set $B=0$ in the hole $|y|<l$ if $l>0$, and assign arbitrary values obeying the same bounds on annular interfaces and the transverse axis. These exceptional positions have zero Lebesgue measure. In particular, for every $k\geq0$, <a id="dil:background-bound"></a>$$\label{dil:background-bound}
\int\langle p\rangle^kB(t,x,\,\mathrm dp)
\leq\frac{C_k}{t+\mu}\leq\frac{C_k}{t}
\quad(t>0)$$ on an active annulus. If $l>0$, the bound is instead uniformly $C_k/(t+l)$ for all $t\geq0$.

<a id="stmt-prop-blowup-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:blowup-limit" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:limit-equation"></a>
**Proposition 20**. *Every sequence <a href="#eq:rescaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rescaling">[eq:rescaling]</a> has a subsequence for which $h_{r_N}\rightharpoonup g$ in local spacetime $L^1$ on $(0,U)\times\mathbb R^3_x\times\mathbb R^3_v$, where $$\label{eq:limit-equation}
0\leq g(t,x,v)\leq(t+l)H(v),\qquad
D_tg=2P(B,g)-g\nu_B.$$ Here $B$ is <a href="#eq:background" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:background">[eq:background]</a>; its gain and loss are defined by integration against the velocity measure. Both terms on the right are locally integrable, with a velocity majorant uniform in position on each compact positive-time interval. Compactly supported velocity averages of $h_{r_N}$ converge strongly in local $L^1_{t,x}$. The limit has a locally strongly continuous $L^1$ representative at positive times and obeys the integrating-factor formula <a href="#dil:mild" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:mild">[dil:mild]</a> below.*

<a id="dil:initial-trace"></a>
*If $l>0$, pass also to a limit $\lambda_N\to\lambda_0$ of the shot parameters. The conclusion and its local continuity extend to time zero, with $$\label{dil:initial-trace}
g(0,x,v)=\lambda_0 l\,\chi(x/l,v).$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Cold convergence: survival, Jacobian, and stripe averaging

<a id="dil:cold-moment-bound"></a>
We first identify the cold measure independently of the hot limit. Fix a compact time-space region $$a\leq t\leq A<U,\qquad |x|\leq R,$$ where $a>0$. Since $U_N\to U$ and $A<U$, eventually $r_NA<T_N$. The cap therefore holds on the entire initial physical interval $[0,r_NA]$, as required for the survival estimate below. By Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a>, the dilated cold velocity moments are bounded there, and indeed uniformly in position, by $$\label{dil:cold-moment-bound}
\int\langle p\rangle^k C_{r_N}(t,x,p)\,\mathrm dp
\leq C_k\left(\frac1{t+l_N}+1\right).$$ The escaped tails have the still smaller bound $C_M r_N^{M+1}t^Me^{-4|p|^2}$ for any fixed $M>0$. Uniformly decreasing velocity tails hold under these moment bounds.

First restrict to annuli with $\mu_{N,j}$ in a fixed compact interval $[\varepsilon,R_1]\subset(0,\infty)$. There are only finitely many of them, uniformly in $N$. Apply Corollary <a href="#jet:survival" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:survival">15</a> at physical time $r_Nt$ to a color of scale $s=r_N\mu_{N,j}$. Its optical depth is bounded, uniformly for $0\le t\le A$, by $C_A(r_Nd_s/s+r_N^2+r_Ns_N)+C_{A,M}r_N^{M+1}$. Here $$\frac{r_Nd_s}{s}=d_0r_N^{10}\mu_{N,j}^{9},\qquad
r_N^2+r_Ns_N\leq2r_N^2,$$ so the corollary gives the same-color comparison $$e^{-\epsilon_N}c_{i,\mathrm{free}}\leq c_i
\leq c_{i,\mathrm{free}}+C(d_s/s)m_i,
\qquad \epsilon_N\longrightarrow0$$ uniformly on these scales. The lower bound covers the full free core because its velocity parameter lies in the compact support of $J$. After multiplication by $r_N$, the remainder has velocity mass <a id="dil:remainder-power"></a>$$\label{dil:remainder-power}
r_N O(d_s/s^2)=O(d_0r_N^9\mu_{N,j}^8).$$ The core moment bound and this estimate show that the dilated actual color and its free core differ by $o(1)$ in velocity total variation, uniformly on compact regions of these annuli. The same holds with each fixed polynomial velocity weight, using compact support of the free-core parameter and the Gaussian remainder.

<a id="dil:free-core-integral"></a>
We compute the free core exactly. Fix one sign and one such annulus, write $\mu_N=s/r_N$, and use the velocity parameters $$\bar p=d_s\bar w,\qquad
p_3=\sigma+\alpha\tanh\frac{\gamma(z-tp_3)}{\mu_N}+d_sw_3.$$ The axial map depends only on $w_3$. Its derivative is $$\frac{\partial p_3}{\partial w_3}
=\frac{d_s}
 {1+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu_N)/\mu_N}.$$ Thus Equation <a href="#eq:core-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-jacobian">[eq:core-jacobian]</a>, after dilation, says that the velocity integral of the free core against a test $\psi$ is $$\label{dil:free-core-integral}
\begin{split}
\int r_N c_{i,\mathrm{free}}(r_Nt,r_Nx,p)\psi(p)\,\mathrm dp
={}&\int J(w)
 \frac{K S(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu_N)}
      {\mu_N+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu_N)}
 \psi(d_s\bar w,p_3)\\
&\quad\cdot\mathbf 1_E(r_Ny-r_Ntd_s\bar w)
 \mathbf 1_{\{|r_N(z-tp_3)|<1/8\}}\,\mathrm dw.
\end{split}$$ On the compact regions under consideration the last axial indicator equals one for all sufficiently large $N$. Since the implicit velocity map has derivative at least one in $p_3$, $$|p_3-u_{\mu_N,\sigma}(t,z)|\leq d_s|w_3|.$$ After the mesh subselection, $\mu_N\to\mu>0$, so the velocity test and the smooth amplitude in <a href="#dil:free-core-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:free-core-integral">[dil:free-core-integral]</a> converge uniformly for $w\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}J$ to $$\psi(u_{\mu,\sigma}e_3)
\frac{K S(\gamma(z-tu_{\mu,\sigma})/\mu)}
 {\mu+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tu_{\mu,\sigma})/\mu)}.$$

It remains to average the transverse indicator. In dilated coordinates its period is $$\frac{s^2}{r_N}=r_N\mu_N^2\longrightarrow0.$$ The transverse shift of its argument is $td_s\bar w$, whose ratio to that period is at most $$\frac{Ad_s}{r_N\mu_N^2}=A d_0 r_N^9\mu_N^8\longrightarrow0.$$ For the indicator of the fixed periodic stripe pattern, a translation by this vanishing relative amount changes its integral on a bounded set by $o(1)$, uniformly in its phase and in bounded $t,w$. One can verify this by summing the lengths of the small intervals around stripe endpoints. The annular margins have dilated width $O(s^2/r_N)=O(r_N\mu_N^2)$, so their area also tends to zero on the annulus. The outer annular radii converge; their boundaries can likewise be removed in sets of arbitrarily small area.

Finally the periodic stripe indicators converge weak-\* to their mean $\theta$. Explicitly, if $q$ is the one-period indicator and $q-\theta$ has bounded periodic primitive $Q$, then for a smooth compactly supported one-dimensional test $f$, period $p$, and arbitrary phase $\zeta$, $$\left|\int f(y_1)(q(y_1/p+\zeta)-\theta)\,\mathrm dy_1\right|
\leq p\|Q\|_\infty\|f'\|_1.$$ Fubini’s theorem gives the same assertion with the other coordinates and parameters present. Approximation in $L^1$ extends it to the annular restrictions. Integrating $J$, whose mass is one, in <a href="#dil:free-core-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:free-core-integral">[dil:free-core-integral]</a> produces exactly the coefficient $b_{\mu,\sigma}$ in <a href="#eq:background" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:background">[eq:background]</a>.

<a id="dil:cold-weakstar"></a>
These arguments identify the cold limit on all compact positive scale annuli. To remove the lower scale restriction, use <a href="#dil:cold-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:cold-moment-bound">[dil:cold-moment-bound]</a>: on the slab $t\geq a$, the integral over $|y|<C\varepsilon$, with bounded $z$, is at most $C_{a,A,R,k}\varepsilon^2$. The same estimate holds for the candidate $B$. Scales larger than a fixed multiple of $R$ have no main color in the chosen spatial compact set, and their escaped tails vanish. We have proved that for every continuous compactly supported velocity test $\psi$, $$\label{dil:cold-weakstar}
\int C_{r_N}(t,x,p)\psi(p)\,\mathrm dp
\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup}
\int\psi(p)B(t,x,\,\mathrm dp)
\quad\hbox{in local }L^\infty_{t,x}$$ on $(0,U)\times\mathbb R^3$. Indeed the computation first gives weak convergence against smooth compact time-space tests, and the common $L^\infty$ bound extends it to all $L^1$ tests. Polynomial velocity tails can also be removed uniformly. If $l>0$, choose $\varepsilon<l/2$; all the bounds used above hold down to time zero, so <a href="#dil:cold-weakstar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:cold-weakstar">[dil:cold-weakstar]</a> does too.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### The hot equation and the size of every error

<a id="dil:prelimit-equation"></a>
<a id="dil:error-bounds"></a>
Under <a href="#eq:rescaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rescaling">[eq:rescaling]</a>, the derivative of $r_N^{-1}h_N(r_Nt,r_Nx,v)$ is the old transport derivative of $h_N$. Therefore the rescaled equation is exactly $$\label{dil:prelimit-equation}
D_th_{r_N}
=2P^{b_N}(C_{r_N},h_{r_N})
 -h_{r_N}\nu^{b_N}_{C_{r_N}}+E_N,$$ where $$\begin{split}
E_N={}&r_N^2\bigl[P^{b_N}(h_{r_N},h_{r_N})
                  -h_{r_N}\nu^{b_N}_{h_{r_N}}\bigr]+S_N,\\
S_N(t,x,v)={}&\sum_{i\ne j}
 P^{b_N}(c_{i,N},c_{j,N})(r_Nt,r_Nx,v).
\end{split}$$ There is no additional factor in $S_N$. Its smallness comes from the physical mixed-cold source estimate: for every sufficiently large fixed $M$, $$\label{dil:error-bounds}
\begin{split}
0\leq S_N(t,x,v)&\leq C_M r_N^{M-1}t^{M-1}H(v),\\
r_N^2P^{b_N}(h_{r_N},h_{r_N})
&\leq C r_N^2(t+l_N)^2H(v),\\
r_N^2h_{r_N}\nu^{b_N}_{h_{r_N}}
&\leq C r_N^2(t+l_N)^2\langle v\rangle H(v).
\end{split}$$ The second line uses Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a>; the last line uses the first velocity moment of $H$. Thus on every fixed compact time interval, $E_N\to0$ in local spacetime $L^1$, with an error bounded by a constant times $r_N^2+r_N^{M-1}$ after integration over a fixed spatial compact set. Choose $M>1$.

<a id="dil:transport-majorant"></a>
The main cold sheets and Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> also give $$P^{b_N}(C_{r_N},H)\leq
 C\left(\frac1{t+l_N}+1\right)H,
\qquad
\nu^{b_N}_{C_{r_N}}(v)\leq
 C\left(\frac1{t+l_N}+1\right)\langle v\rangle.$$ The escaped cold component is bounded by a vanishing multiple of $e^{-4|v|^2}\leq CH$, so its gain is covered by Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a>. Equations <a href="#dil:cap-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:cap-bound">[dil:cap-bound]</a> and <a href="#dil:prelimit-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:prelimit-equation">[dil:prelimit-equation]</a> now imply, on $a\leq t\leq A<U$, $$\label{dil:transport-majorant}
|D_th_{r_N}(t,x,v)|\leq C_{a,A}\langle v\rangle H(v).$$ This is a genuine integrable velocity majorant, uniform in position. If $l>0$, the same conclusion holds on $0\leq t\leq A$, since $l_N\geq l/2$ eventually.

The cap gives weak compactness of $h_{r_N}$ in local $L^1$, and <a href="#dil:transport-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:transport-majorant">[dil:transport-majorant]</a> permits application of Lemma <a href="#comp:averaging" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:averaging">19</a>. Subselect on an exhaustion of $(0,U)\times\mathbb R^3$. The resulting limit $g$ satisfies the bound in <a href="#eq:limit-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-equation">[eq:limit-equation]</a>, and each compactly supported velocity average converges strongly in local $L^1_{t,x}$.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Passing the cold–hot products

The dilated cold densities concentrate in velocity, so the two-density compactness argument of Section <a href="#sec:admissibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:admissibility">6</a> does not apply to $C_{r_N}$. The needed product passage uses <a href="#dil:cold-weakstar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:cold-weakstar">[dil:cold-weakstar]</a> and hot averaging instead.

<a id="dil:mixed-product"></a>
For compact velocity tests $\psi,\chi$, put $$a_N(t,x)=\int\psi(p)C_{r_N}(t,x,p)\,\mathrm dp,\qquad
\zeta_N(t,x)=\int\chi(v)h_{r_N}(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv.$$ On every compact positive-time region, $a_N$ is bounded in $L^\infty$ and converges weak-\* to $a=\int\psi\,\mathrm dB$, while $\zeta_N\to\zeta=\int\chi g\,\mathrm dv$ strongly in $L^1$. For a bounded time-space test $\varphi$, $$\int\varphi(a_N\zeta_N-a\zeta)
=\int\varphi a_N(\zeta_N-\zeta)
 +\int\varphi(a_N-a)\zeta\longrightarrow0.$$ Finite sums of separated velocity tests approximate continuous tests uniformly on compact velocity products. The total product mass is uniformly bounded on compact time-space regions by <a href="#dil:cold-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:cold-moment-bound">[dil:cold-moment-bound]</a> and the cap. We conclude $$\label{dil:mixed-product}
C_{r_N}(t,x,p)h_{r_N}(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dp\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dt
\rightharpoonup B(t,x,\,\mathrm dp)g(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dt$$ against continuous compact velocity tests, with arbitrary smooth compact time-space dependence. Uniform moment bounds in both velocities allow removal of velocity cutoffs in tests with any fixed polynomial growth for which they are used below.

Here is an explicit weak collision test suitable for this convergence. For a continuous output test $\phi$, let $$\mathcal K_\phi(p,v)
=\frac{|p-v|}{2}\int_{S^2}
 \phi\left(\frac{p+v}{2}+\frac{|p-v|}{2}\omega\right)\,\mathrm d\omega.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:carleman">3</a> gives $\int\phi P(c,h)=\iint c(p)h(v)\mathcal K_\phi(p,v)\,\mathrm dp\,\mathrm dv$. The test $\mathcal K_\phi$ is continuous in both incoming velocities, including $p=v$, and is bounded in absolute value by $2\pi|p-v|\|\phi\|_\infty$. The loss test is $2\pi|p-v|\phi(v)$, with the same properties. Inserting smooth time-space dependence in $\phi$ does not change these facts. On bounded velocity products the factor $\mathbf 1_{\{|p-v|<b_N\}}$ is eventually one; its complement is uniformly negligible by the weighted product tails. Applying <a href="#dil:mixed-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:mixed-product">[dil:mixed-product]</a> proves the required gain and loss limits.

The limiting gain is an integrable function, rather than an additional singular output measure. Indeed each summand of $B$ is an allowed zero-width axial sheet in Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a>; hence on $a\leq t\leq A$, $$0\leq P(B,g)\leq C_{a,A}H,
\qquad 0\leq g\nu_B\leq C_{a,A}\langle v\rangle H.$$ Equivalently the weak gain measure is dominated by this integrable function, which also verifies absolute continuity at the velocity axis. Passing <a href="#dil:prelimit-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:prelimit-equation">[dil:prelimit-equation]</a>, using <a href="#dil:error-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:error-bounds">[dil:error-bounds]</a>, proves the equation in <a href="#eq:limit-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-equation">[eq:limit-equation]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### A positive lower mesh endpoint and the local mild formula

When $l>0$, <a href="#dil:transport-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:transport-majorant">[dil:transport-majorant]</a> holds uniformly down to zero. The rescaled initial hot density, on every fixed spatial compact set for large $N$, is exactly $$h_{r_N}(0,x,v)=\lambda_N l_N\chi(x/l_N,v).$$ The other periodic copies have moved outside that compact set. Subselect $\lambda_N\to\lambda_0$. Smoothness and compact support of $\chi$, and $l_N\to l>0$, give strong local $L^1$ convergence to <a href="#dil:initial-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dil:initial-trace">[dil:initial-trace]</a>. Passing the weak transport identity with tests touching time zero is justified by the uniform source majorant and yields that initial trace for $g$. The cold product argument also extends to zero in this case, as already noted. For $l=0$ the coefficient can be of order $1/t$, and the proposition only asserts the equation on the open positive-time interval.

<a id="dil:mild"></a>
On any $[a,A]\subset(0,U)$, the limiting transport source is locally integrable and has the preceding velocity majorant uniformly in position. Changing to free coordinates as in <a href="#comp:path-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:path-formula">[comp:path-formula]</a>, first on bounded velocities and enlarged spatial compact sets, gives a locally strongly continuous $L^1$ representative. The uniform integrable velocity tails then remove the velocity truncation. The cap bound passes to every trace of this representative. Almost every free path obeys the scalar absolutely continuous equation $D_tg+\nu_Bg=2P(B,g)$. Its loss coefficient is locally integrable along the path, since $\nu_B(t,x,v)\leq C_a\langle v\rangle$ for $t\geq a$. The integrating factor therefore gives, for $0<a<t<U$, $$\label{dil:mild}
\begin{split}
g(t,x,v)={}&g(a,x-(t-a)v,v)
 \exp\left[-\int_a^t\nu_B(\tau,x-(t-\tau)v,v)\,\mathrm d\tau\right]\\
&+2\int_a^t P(B,g)(s,x-(t-s)v,v)
 \exp\left[-\int_s^t\nu_B(\tau,x-(t-\tau)v,v)\,\mathrm d\tau\right]\,\mathrm ds.
\end{split}$$ This is an almost-everywhere identity, or equivalently an identity in local $L^1$ at the displayed times. It holds with $a=0$ when $l>0$. Altering the medium on the null annular interfaces does not change the identity: free transport preserves phase-space measure, so Fubini’s theorem discards the affected set of paths. These statements complete the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:blowup-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blowup-limit">20</a>.

<a id="nc:section"></a>
### A cap event produces a nonzero limit

The weak limit in Proposition <a href="#prop:blowup-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blowup-limit">20</a> is obtained on an open time interval. A supremum attained by an approximation could in principle disappear through concentration, or through escape from that interval. The purpose of this section is to exclude both possibilities. All constants used below may depend on the fixed jet parameters. None depends on the approximation index.

<a id="stmt-prop-nonzero-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:nonzero-limit" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Nonvanishing of every cap limit). *Consider any sequence of cap shots with horizons $T_n\to0$, approximation indices $N_n\to\infty$, and $s_{N_n}/T_n\to0$. Make the rescaling <a href="#eq:rescaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rescaling">[eq:rescaling]</a>, and pass to any subsequence supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:blowup-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blowup-limit">20</a>. Its limit satisfies $$g\not\equiv0\qquad\text{on }(0,U)\times\mathbb R^3_x\times\mathbb R^3_v.$$ This conclusion includes $l=1$, $U=1$, and $U=\infty$.*

</div>

We first describe the positive kernels used in the proof. Subsequently we establish the two different mechanisms needed near and away from the velocity axis.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### A positive majorizing equation and its kernels

<a id="nc:cap-normalized"></a>
For this section relabel the sequence by $n$, set $U_n=T_n/r_n$, and write $$r_n=t_{*,n}+s_{N_n},\quad
 \ell_n=\frac{s_{N_n}}{r_n},\quad
 \tau_n=\frac{t_{*,n}}{r_n}=1-\ell_n,\quad
 h_n=h_{r_n},\quad C_n=C_{r_n},\quad f_n=\frac{h_n}{H}.$$ Then $\ell_n\to l$. In particular $0\le\ell_n\le1$, $0\le\tau_n\le1$. The cap gives, on $0\le t\le U_n$, $$\label{nc:cap-normalized}
 0\le f_n(t,x,v)\le t+\ell_n,\qquad
 \left\|f_n(\tau_n)\right\|_\infty=1.$$ Essential supremum norms and pointwise formulas involving $H$ always ignore the velocity axis, a null set. The mild representatives of the finite approximations will be used at the cap times. Choose Borel representatives when composing densities with the maps below, enforcing their asserted bounds on exceptional null sets as well. Characteristic identities and endpoint choices are still made only on their common full-measure sets.

<a id="stmt-nc-core-kernels"></a>
<div id="nc:core-kernels" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Core kernels on the dilated scale). *There are nonnegative velocity measures $A_n(t,x,\,\mathrm dp)$ and numbers $\varepsilon_n\to0$ with the following properties. After discarding finitely many terms so that $r_n\le\overline T$, the measures $A_n$ are defined from free transport on the whole interval $0\le t\le1$, independently of the cap shot’s existence interval. The bounds on $A_n$ below hold on that whole interval; comparison with $h_n$ is used only while the shot exists.*

<a id="nc:positive-equation"></a>
1.  *On $0<t<\min(1,U_n)$, along almost every free characteristic, $$\label{nc:positive-equation}
     D_th_n\le 2P(A_n,h_n)+\varepsilon_n H .$$ At each point $A_n$ contains at most one main free core. Its dilated scale $\mu=s/r_n$ satisfies $|y|\asymp\mu$, with coordinates measured from its periodic copy, and $$\frac{2P(A_n,H)}{H}\le\frac{C}{t+\ell_n}.$$*

<a id="nc:core-parameters"></a>
2.  *Before spatial masks are imposed, a core of scale $\mu=s/r_n$ and sign $\sigma$ is the pushforward of $$\label{nc:core-parameters}
     \frac{K S\bigl(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu\bigr)}
          {\mu+t\alpha\gamma S\bigl(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu\bigr)}
     J(w)\,\mathrm dw ,
     \qquad
     \bar p=d_s\bar w,\quad
     p_3=\sigma+\alpha\tanh\bigl(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu\bigr)+d_sw_3 .$$ The actual masks are measurable factors between zero and one. The density in <a href="#nc:core-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:core-parameters">[nc:core-parameters]</a> is at most $C(t+\mu)^{-1}J(w)$.*

<a id="nc:normalized-kernel"></a>
3.  *For $t\ge a>0$, the normalized gain measure $$\label{nc:normalized-kernel}
     \mathcal K_n(t,x,v;\,\mathrm dp\,\,\mathrm d\xi)
     =
     4A_n(t,x,\,\mathrm dp)\,
     \delta\bigl((v-p)\cdot(\xi-v)\bigr)
     \frac{H(\xi)}{H(v)}\,\mathrm d\xi$$ has total mass at most $C/a$. Uniformly in its base point, the contributions from large $|p|+|\xi-v|$, or from large retained Gaussian parameters in a product-sheet majorant, tend to zero. Its total mass tends uniformly to zero as $|v|\to\infty$. The axial interval, axial mismatch, and small transverse input conclusions of Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-tightness">7</a> apply to $\mathcal K_n$, with the amplitude factor $C/a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-core-kernels"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-core-kernels</summary>

*Proof.* For $0\le t\le1$, let $i$ be the retained label whose enlargement $\mathcal O_i(r_nt)$ contains $r_nx$, if there is one, and define $$A_n(t,x,\,\mathrm dp)=r_n f_i(r_nt,r_nx,p)\,\mathrm dp;$$ set $A_n=0$ when no such label exists. The free cores $f_i$ and enlargements are defined from the datum, so this definition does not require a cap shot beyond $U_n$. Their disjointness and free-core bounds hold because $r_nt\le\overline T$.

On $0\le t\le\min(1,U_n)$, Proposition <a href="#prop:cold-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cold-control">14</a> compares the actual cold density with these free cores. All other free cores belong to the escaped sum. The main remainder has, after dilation, integrated sheet amplitude at most $$C r_n\frac{d_s}{s^2}=C r_nd_0s^8\le C r_n .$$ The escaped sum is bounded by $C_A r_n(r_nt)^A e^{-4|v|^2}$. Use Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> for the main narrow remainder. For the escaped term, $e^{-4|v|^2}\le CH(v)$ and Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a> give the required gain bound. Together these supply a positive remainder $R_n$ with $C_n\le A_n+R_n$ and $P(R_n,H)/H=o(1)$, uniformly on that interval. There the cap gives $h_n\le2H$.

In the dilated hot equation the hot–hot gain is $r_n^2P(h_n,h_n)$, bounded by $Cr_n^2H$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a>. The mixed cold source is bounded by $C_A(r_nt)^{A-1}H$; choose $A>1$. Ignore all losses and combine these bounds with the remainder just described. This proves <a href="#nc:positive-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:positive-equation">[nc:positive-equation]</a> with $\varepsilon_n\to0$, uniformly down to time zero.

Equation <a href="#nc:core-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:core-parameters">[nc:core-parameters]</a> is <a href="#eq:core-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-jacobian">[eq:core-jacobian]</a> after multiplying the cold density by $r_n$. Since the smallest scale is $\ell_n$, its amplitude is bounded by $C/(t+\ell_n)$. The geometric restriction comes from the enlarged transverse set of the main color; its relative enlargement is $O(s)$, uniformly small.

The coefficient $4$ in <a href="#nc:normalized-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:normalized-kernel">[nc:normalized-kernel]</a> is the product of the coefficient $2$ in the linear hot gain and the coefficient $2$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:carleman">3</a>. The gain and velocity-tail bounds follow from Lemmas <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> and <a href="#lem:kernel-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-tightness">7</a>. For parameter truncations, apply Lemma <a href="#ker:parameter-sheets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ker:parameter-sheets">8</a> with the common bounds $A=1+\alpha$, $C_0=C_J$, and the physical width $d_s$. The coefficient $C/(t+\mu)\le C/a$ on this slab remains a domination amplitude outside the normalized parameter law. The parameter-tail estimate controls the complete normalized gain, including $H(\xi)/H(v)$, uniformly under the axial compression in <a href="#nc:core-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:core-parameters">[nc:core-parameters]</a>. For the exact free core, $J$ already has bounded parameter support. ◻

</details>

For a fixed $a>0$, define the backward Volterra operator <a id="nc:volterra"></a>$$\label{nc:volterra}
 (\mathcal V_n F)(t,x,v)
 =
 \int_a^t\int
 F\bigl(s,x-(t-s)v,\xi\bigr)
 \mathcal K_n\bigl(s,x-(t-s)v,v;\,\mathrm dp\,\,\mathrm d\xi\bigr)\,\mathrm ds .$$ *Representatives in the finite-temperature iteration.* For each finite $n$, the core measure has a velocity density, $A_n(s,x,\,\mathrm dp)=a_n(s,x,p)\,\mathrm dp$. Integrating first in $p$ in <a href="#nc:normalized-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:normalized-kernel">[nc:normalized-kernel]</a> shows that, for $\xi\ne v$, its input marginal is $$\mathcal K_n(s,x,v;\mathbb R^3,\,\mathrm d\xi)
=\frac{4H(\xi)}{|\xi-v|H(v)}
  \left[\int_{(v-p)\cdot(\xi-v)=0}
       a_n(s,x,p)\,\mathrm dA_p\right]\,\mathrm d\xi.$$ There is no atom at $\xi=v$: each plane-area measure gives that point zero mass, and $p=v$ is null for the cold density. For a null set in $(s,X,\xi)$, integrate also in the endpoint variables and use the measure-preserving change $X=x-(t-s)v$. Tonelli’s theorem then shows that altering the input on that set changes $\mathcal V_nF(t)$ only on a null set of endpoints, at each fixed terminal time. Finite repetition preserves this property. A countable sequence of lower times $a\downarrow0$ suffices below; intersecting the good endpoint sets for these times, the approximation indices, and the finitely many iterations gives the common full-measure sets used at the cap events.

<a id="nc:iteration-norm"></a>
Positivity and Lemma <a href="#nc:core-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:core-kernels">22</a> give, for a bounded nonnegative $F$, $$\label{nc:iteration-norm}
 \|\mathcal V_n^jF(t)\|_\infty
 \le \|F\|_\infty\frac{\bigl(C\log(t/a)\bigr)^j}{j!},
 \qquad a\le t\le1.$$ Indeed, the ordered $j$-time integral of $\prod_{i=1}^j C/t_i$ equals $(C\log(t/a))^j/j!$. In particular every fixed number of further gains has bounded mass on this slab.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Reduction to a finite gain integral

We begin the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:nonzero-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:nonzero-limit">21</a>. The case $l>0$ is settled directly. For $l=0$, the cap will force a positive contribution from five successive gains; the remaining subsections show that this contribution vanishes if the weak limit is zero.

Pass to a subsequence on which the shot parameters converge, $\lambda_n\to\lambda_\infty$. If $l>0$ and $\lambda_\infty>0$, Proposition <a href="#prop:blowup-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blowup-limit">20</a> gives the initial hot trace $$g(0,x,v)=\lambda_\infty l\,\chi(x/l,v).$$ This trace is nonzero. More explicitly, the limiting loss frequency obeys $$\nu_B(t,x,v)\le
       \frac{C(1+|v|)}{t+l}.$$ On any bounded velocity set and any finite interval beginning at zero its integral along a flight is finite. The positive mild formula for <a href="#eq:limit-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-equation">[eq:limit-equation]</a> therefore retains the initial trace multiplied by a strictly positive survival factor. A bounded positive portion of the smooth nonzero bump gives positive interior mass.

If $l>0$ and $\lambda_\infty=0$, use Lemma <a href="#nc:core-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:core-kernels">22</a> from time zero. The initial ratio has norm at most $\lambda_n\ell_n\|\chi/H\|_\infty$, independently of the spatial scaling of the bump. On $0\le t\le\tau_n\le1$, eventually $t+\ell_n\ge l/2$. The positive integral inequality and the elementary scalar integrating-factor estimate give $$\sup_{0\le t\le\tau_n}\|f_n(t)\|_\infty
 \le
 \bigl(\lambda_n\ell_n\|\chi/H\|_\infty+\varepsilon_n\bigr)
 e^{2C/l}\longrightarrow0.$$ This contradicts <a href="#nc:cap-normalized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:cap-normalized">[nc:cap-normalized]</a>. Both alternatives also apply when $l=1$, even if $\tau_n\to0$ or some hit occurs at time zero.

<a id="nc:zero-in-measure"></a>
It remains to consider $l=0$, so $\tau_n\to1$. Suppose, to obtain a contradiction, that $g=0$ throughout the open interval $(0,U)$. Nonnegativity upgrades this zero weak limit to local $L^1$ convergence: if $K$ is a compact subset of the open spacetime–velocity region, take a nonnegative smooth compact test at least one on $K$. Its pairing with $h_n$ tends to zero, hence $\int_K h_n\to0$. Away from the velocity axis $H$ has a positive lower bound on compact velocity sets. Consequently $$\label{nc:zero-in-measure}
 f_n\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{in measure on compact subsets of }
 (0,U)\times\mathbb R^3_x\times\{\bar v\ne0\}.$$

<a id="nc:five-residual"></a>
Fix $0<a<1/4$, so that $a<\tau_n$ for large $n$. The positive mild inequality <a href="#nc:positive-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:positive-equation">[nc:positive-equation]</a> reads $$f_n\le d_n+\mathcal V_nf_n+e_n,$$ where $d_n(t,x,v)=
 f_n(a,x-(t-a)v,v)$, $\|d_n\|_\infty\le a+\ell_n$, and $0\le e_n\le\varepsilon_n$ on this unit-length interval. Iterate the gain term five times. Using positivity and <a href="#nc:iteration-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:iteration-norm">[nc:iteration-norm]</a> gives $$\label{nc:five-residual}
 f_n(t,x,v)
 \le
 (a+\ell_n+\varepsilon_n)
 \sum_{j=0}^4\frac{(C\log(1/a))^j}{j!}
 +(\mathcal V_n^5 f_n)(t,x,v),
 \qquad a\le t\le\tau_n .$$ Every formula is used on a common full-measure set of characteristics for the finite approximation.

The term outside $\mathcal V_n^5f_n$ becomes arbitrarily small when first $n\to\infty$ and then $a\downarrow0$. We therefore need to show that the five-fold term tends to zero at endpoints carrying a fixed positive fraction of the cap. We first control those endpoints, then separate axial and nonaxial limiting velocities.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Compactness of the relevant endpoints

<a id="stmt-nc-endpoint-tightness"></a>
<div id="nc:endpoint-tightness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Endpoint and path truncations). *Fix $a>0$, and let $t_n\in[a,1]$. In a five-fold integral $(\mathcal V_n^5F_n)(t_n,x_n,v_n)$, assume $0\le F_n\le2$. For every prescribed error, one may restrict all $|p_i|+|v_i-v_{i-1}|$ and sheet parameters to fixed bounded sets at that error, uniformly in $n,t_n,x_n,v_n$. Moreover:*

1.  *if $|v_n|\to\infty$, the whole integral tends to zero;*

2.  *after retaining bounded endpoint velocities, an endpoint escaping every bounded neighborhood of every dilated periodic origin also has a vanishing integral.*

*Consequently any sequence along which the five-fold integral has a positive lower bound has, after periodic translations and subselection, convergent endpoint positions and velocities.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-endpoint-tightness"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-endpoint-tightness</summary>

*Proof.* At any one gain, a removed normalized tail is uniformly small by Lemma <a href="#nc:core-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:core-kernels">22</a>. Bound the remaining four gains and the input $F_n$ by <a href="#nc:iteration-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:iteration-norm">[nc:iteration-norm]</a>. Summing over the five possible locations of a removed tail proves the first assertion. The same argument at the first gain, using its uniform decay for large outgoing $v_n$, proves (i).

On the retained paths all velocities are bounded by a fixed number, and the sum of free-flight lengths is at most that number. Thus every collision position is a bounded dilated distance from the endpoint. If its transverse distance from every periodic origin tends to infinity, each encountered main scale satisfies $\mu=s/r_n\to\infty$, or no core is encountered. The bound $C/(t+\mu)\le C/\mu$ makes the first nonzero core kernel negligible.

Suppose instead that the transverse coordinate is bounded relative to a periodic origin while the axial coordinate escapes all such origins. Encountered main scales satisfy $0<\mu\le M$. For bounded $p_3,t$, their amplitudes obey <a id="nc:axial-escape"></a>$$\label{nc:axial-escape}
 \frac{K S(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu)}
      {\mu+t\alpha\gamma S(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu)}
 \le \frac K\mu S(\gamma(z-tp_3)/\mu)\longrightarrow0$$ uniformly in $0<\mu\le M$ as $|z|\to\infty$. For example, when $|z-tp_3|\ge Z$, the right side is at most $4K\mu^{-1}e^{-2\gamma Z/\mu}$; its supremum over $0<\mu\le M$ tends to zero. The core masks can only decrease these bounds. The same estimates hold for the first gain of every retained path, and the remaining gains have uniformly bounded mass.

The period of the dilated density is $1/r_n\to\infty$. Choose a nearest periodic origin before extracting the endpoint subsequence. A path of bounded dilated length cannot visit neighborhoods of two different such origins for large $n$. The two preceding alternatives cover failure of boundedness relative to the chosen origin, and prove (ii). ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### Two gains at an axial endpoint

For $\mu>0$ and $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}$, denote by $u_{\mu,\sigma}(t,z)$ the solution of $$u=\sigma+\alpha\tanh\bigl(\gamma(z-tu)/\mu\bigr).$$ This notation extends the profile independently of every annular or stripe mask.

<a id="stmt-nc-characteristic-matching"></a>
<div id="nc:characteristic-matching" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Characteristic matching and small scales). *On every compact set with $t\ge a>0$, the following statements hold uniformly.*

<a id="nc:matching-inequality"></a>
1.  *If $a\le t_2\le t_1$, $\Delta=t_1-t_2$, and $z_2=z_1-\Delta w$, then $$\label{nc:matching-inequality}
     |w-u_{\mu,\sigma}(t_1,z_1)|
     \le |w-u_{\mu,\sigma}(t_2,z_2)|.$$*

<a id="nc:thermal-speed-error"></a>
2.  *As $\mu\downarrow0$, the distance of $u_{\mu,\sigma}(t,z)$ to $$\{z/t,\ \sigma-\alpha,\ \sigma+\alpha\}$$ tends to zero uniformly. For the perturbed axial velocity in <a href="#nc:core-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:core-parameters">[nc:core-parameters]</a>, $$\label{nc:thermal-speed-error}
     |p_3-u_{\mu,\sigma}(t,z)|\le d_s|w_3|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-characteristic-matching"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-characteristic-matching</summary>

*Proof.* The profile satisfies $$\partial_z u_{\mu,\sigma}
 =\frac{(\alpha\gamma/\mu)S}
        {1+(t\alpha\gamma/\mu)S}\ge0,
 \qquad
 \partial_tu_{\mu,\sigma}
       +u_{\mu,\sigma}\partial_zu_{\mu,\sigma}=0.$$ For fixed $t_1,t_2,z_1$, the function $F(w)=w-u_{\mu,\sigma}(t_2,z_1-\Delta w)$ has derivative $F'(w)\ge1$. Its root is $u_{\mu,\sigma}(t_1,z_1)$: the defining implicit equation shows that this velocity is unchanged along the straight segment between the two points. Integrating $F'\ge1$ proves <a href="#nc:matching-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:matching-inequality">[nc:matching-inequality]</a>.

Fix $0<\delta<\alpha$. If $u_{\mu,\sigma}$ is within $\delta$ of an endpoint speed, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise $$\left|\frac{u_{\mu,\sigma}-\sigma}{\alpha}\right|
 \le1-\frac{\delta}{\alpha},
 \qquad
 |z-tu_{\mu,\sigma}|
 \le\frac{\mu}{\gamma}
       \operatorname{arctanh}(1-\delta/\alpha).$$ It follows that $$|u_{\mu,\sigma}-z/t|
 \le\frac{\mu}{\gamma a}
       \operatorname{arctanh}(1-\delta/\alpha).$$ First choose $\delta$, then $\mu$ small. This proves the uniform assertion in (ii). Finally, the function $$p\longmapsto p-\sigma-\alpha\tanh\bigl(\gamma(z-tp)/\mu\bigr)$$ has derivative at least one. Its values at $u_{\mu,\sigma}$ and at the perturbed $p_3$ are $0$ and $d_sw_3$. This proves <a href="#nc:thermal-speed-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:thermal-speed-error">[nc:thermal-speed-error]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-nc-two-axial-gains"></a>
<div id="nc:two-axial-gains" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Vanishing of two axial gains). *Fix $a>0$. Let $t_n\in[a,1]$, and suppose the endpoint positions and velocities are bounded with $\bar v_n\to0$. Then $$(\mathcal V_n^2 1)(t_n,x_n,v_n)\longrightarrow0.$$ The conclusion continues to hold if all subsequent factors in a longer finite gain integral are bounded uniformly.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-two-axial-gains"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-two-axial-gains</summary>

*Proof.* Remove normalized parameter and increment tails at an arbitrarily small cost, using Lemma <a href="#nc:core-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:core-kernels">22</a>. On the retained two-gain paths, all velocities and positions lie in fixed compact sets, the times lie in $[a,1]$, and every active scale satisfies $0<\mu\le M$. In particular the largest possible physical width is $d_0(r_nM)^{10}\to0$.

Write $t_1,x_1=(y_1,z_1)$ for the first collision point, and $\xi$ for its input velocity. The next point has axial coordinate $z_2=z_1-(t_1-t_2)\xi_3$. All estimates below are uniform when $t_1,x_1$ vary in their retained compact sets. At the first gain, Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-tightness">7</a> supplies a common modulus $\omega(\rho)\downarrow0$ for the mass of any axial interval of length $\rho$; the interval’s center is arbitrary. It also says that for each fixed $b>0$, the first-kernel mass on $\{|\bar\xi|>b\}$ tends to zero as $\bar v_n\to0$.

Here is an order of choices that handles all scales at the second point. Fix an error $\epsilon>0$, including the fixed factors from the two time integrations and the bound $C/a$. Choose $\delta>0$ so that the sum of the first-kernel masses of the $\delta$-neighborhoods of the five numbers <a id="nc:collapsed-speeds"></a>$$\label{nc:collapsed-speeds}
 z_1/t_1,\quad 1-\alpha,\quad1+\alpha,\quad
 -1-\alpha,\quad-1+\alpha$$ is at most $\epsilon$. This is possible uniformly because there are five intervals and their lengths are $2\delta$. Lemma <a href="#nc:characteristic-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:characteristic-matching">24</a> then gives $\mu_0>0$, chosen uniformly on the retained compact sets, so that every profile with $\mu<\mu_0$ is within $\delta/4$ of its corresponding three collapsed speeds.

The geometric mesh has a uniformly bounded number $J_0$ of scales in $[\mu_0,M]$. Choose $b_0>0$ so small that the first-kernel mass of the union of the $b_0$-neighborhoods of $$\{u_{\mu,\sigma}(t_1,z_1):
       \mu\in[\mu_0,M]\text{ in the current mesh},\
       \sigma=\pm1\}$$ is at most $\epsilon$. For example it suffices that $2J_0\omega(2b_0)\le\epsilon$, after incorporating the fixed amplitude bound into $\omega$. This list may depend on $n$; its cardinality bound does not. Its centers may depend on $t_1,z_1$, which are held fixed when the first kernel is integrated. Thus neither dependence weakens the interval bound.

Let $E_n(t_1,z_1)$ be the union of these exceptional intervals. Outside $E_n$, every retained cold speed at the second point has an axial mismatch bounded below by a fixed positive number for all large $n$. For $\mu\ge\mu_0$, this follows from <a href="#nc:matching-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:matching-inequality">[nc:matching-inequality]</a> and <a href="#nc:thermal-speed-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:thermal-speed-error">[nc:thermal-speed-error]</a>, with lower bound $b_0/2$. For a constant collapsed speed it follows immediately, with lower bound $\delta/2$. For the remaining branch use the exact identity $$\xi_3-\frac{z_2}{t_2}
 =\frac{t_1}{t_2}
       \left(\xi_3-\frac{z_1}{t_1}\right);$$ the factor $t_1/t_2\ge1$ preserves the lower bound. The errors $\delta/4$ in small-scale approximation and the vanishing thermal errors can be absorbed. We have therefore obtained, on the complement of $E_n$, $$|\xi_3-p_3^{(2)}|
 \ge c_*:=\tfrac14\min(\delta,b_0)>0$$ for every retained second-core parameter.

The second-kernel axial mismatch estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-tightness">7</a> now tends uniformly to zero as $|\bar\xi|\to0$. Choose $b>0$ so small that this second kernel has mass at most $\epsilon$ whenever $|\bar\xi|\le b$ and the displayed mismatch holds. For this fixed $b$, the first-kernel mass on $|\bar\xi|>b$ tends to zero. Its mass on $E_n$ is at most $2\epsilon$, while the remaining first-kernel mass is bounded. Combining the three regions, then removing the initial tail error, gives a bound tending to zero as $\epsilon\downarrow0$. All choices preceded the final passage $n\to\infty$, and were uniform in the two times. This proves the lemma.

In a longer integral, all factors following these two gains are bounded by <a href="#nc:iteration-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:iteration-norm">[nc:iteration-norm]</a>; the same proof applies. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Finite-dimensional regularization away from the axis

The next two lemmas make explicit the relation between plane area, redundant angular parameters, and the pullback of sets of small spacetime–velocity volume.

<a id="stmt-nc-plane-parameters"></a>
<div id="nc:plane-parameters" class="lemma">

<a id="nc:plane-identity"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Redundant plane parameters). *Let $n\ne0$, $e=n/|n|$, and let $\zeta$ be a bounded nonnegative function supported in $[-1,1]$ with integral one. For every nonnegative measurable $F$, $$\label{nc:plane-identity}
 \int_{n^\perp} F(k)\frac{\,\mathrm dA(k)}{|n|}
 =
 \int_{\mathbb R^3}F(n\times L)|n|\zeta(L\cdot e)\,\mathrm dL .$$ If $\delta\le|n|\le R$ and $|k|\le R_1$, the right side is supported in $$|L|\le\sqrt{(R_1/\delta)^2+1}$$ and has density at most $R\|\zeta\|_\infty$. The identity can be applied successively when each normal is a measurable function of parameters chosen at earlier gains.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-plane-parameters"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-plane-parameters</summary>

*Proof.* Write $L=L^\perp+\lambda e$. The map $L^\perp\mapsto n\times L^\perp$ multiplies two-dimensional area by $|n|^2$. Integration in $\lambda$ contributes $\int\zeta=1$, proving <a href="#nc:plane-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:plane-identity">[nc:plane-identity]</a> and the support and density bounds. For successive gains, fix all previous parameters before applying the identity. The current normal is then fixed; Fubini’s theorem for nonnegative functions justifies iteration. No derivatives of the variable normals enter this conditional change of variables. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-nc-uniform-pullback"></a>
<div id="nc:uniform-pullback" class="lemma">

<a id="nc:pullback-conclusion"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Uniform pullback estimate). *Let $\mathcal O\subset\mathbb R^k$ be open, $k\ge d$, and suppose $\Phi_n,\Phi:\mathcal O\to\mathbb R^d$ satisfy $\Phi_n\to\Phi$ in $C^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and $\operatorname{rank}D\Phi=d$ almost everywhere. Let $K\Subset\mathcal O$ and $B\subset\mathbb R^d$ be compact. Let $a_n$ be measurable, supported in $K$, with $|a_n|\le A$. Let $F_n$ be measurable, supported in $B$, with $|F_n|\le M$, and converging to zero in measure. Then $$\label{nc:pullback-conclusion}
 \int_{\mathcal O}|F_n(\Phi_n(q))a_n(q)|\,\mathrm dq\longrightarrow0 .$$ No convergence or continuity of the densities $a_n$ is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-uniform-pullback"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-uniform-pullback</summary>

*Proof.* Boundedness and convergence in measure on the finite-volume set $B$ imply $\|F_n\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}\to0$: for every $\eta>0$, bound the integral by $\eta|B|+M|\{|F_n|>\eta\}|$.

Fix $\epsilon>0$. The critical set in $K$ has measure zero, so choose an open neighborhood $E$ of it with $|E\cap K|<\epsilon$. The compact set $K\setminus E$ consists of regular points. At such a point $q_0$, choose $d$ input coordinates giving a nonzero minor of $D\Phi(q_0)$. Let $\pi$ be projection onto the complementary $k-d$ coordinates, and define $$G(q)=(\Phi(q),\pi q),\qquad
 G_n(q)=(\Phi_n(q),\pi q),\qquad A_0=DG(q_0).$$ The matrix $A_0$ is invertible. Choose a convex ball $V\Subset\mathcal O$ about $q_0$ so small that, for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\sup_{q\in V}\|A_0^{-1}DG_n(q)-I\|<\tfrac12.$$ Integration on the line segment between $q,q'\in V$ gives $$|A_0^{-1}(G_n(q)-G_n(q'))-(q-q')|
 \le\tfrac12|q-q'|.$$ Thus $G_n$ is injective on $V$, and its Jacobian has a positive lower bound $c_V$ independent of $n$. The inverse function theorem consequently makes it a diffeomorphism onto its image.

Cover $K\setminus E$ by finitely many such balls $V_j$. The change-of-variables formula gives $$\begin{split}
 \int_{V_j\cap K}|F_n(\Phi_n(q))|\,\mathrm dq
 &\le c_{V_j}^{-1}
       \int_{G_{n,j}(V_j\cap K)}|F_n(z)|\,\mathrm dz\,\,\mathrm dw\\
 &\le c_{V_j}^{-1}|\pi_j(V_j)|
       \|F_n\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}.
 \end{split}$$ When $k=d$, the zero-dimensional factor has value one. The integral on $E\cap K$, including its density, is at most $AM\epsilon$. Sum over the finite cover, pass to the limit in $n$, and then let $\epsilon\downarrow0$. This proves <a href="#nc:pullback-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:pullback-conclusion">[nc:pullback-conclusion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-five-gain-rank"></a>
<div id="lem:five-gain-rank" class="lemma">

<a id="nc:rank-map"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Five-gain rank and robust pullback). *Fix a nonaxial endpoint velocity $v_0$, an endpoint $(t_0,x_0)$ with $t_0>0$, five positive scales $\mu_i$, and five signs $\sigma_i$. Given positive times $t_i$ and $L_i\in\mathbb R^3$, define recursively $$\label{nc:rank-map}
 \begin{split}
 x_i&=x_{i-1}-(t_{i-1}-t_i)v_{i-1},\\
 p_i&=u_{\mu_i,\sigma_i}(t_i,z_i)e_3,\qquad
 n_i=v_{i-1}-p_i,\\
 k_i&=n_i\times L_i,\qquad v_i=v_{i-1}+k_i ,
 \qquad i=1,\ldots,5 .
 \end{split}$$ The map $$\Phi:(t_1,\ldots,t_5,L_1,\ldots,L_5)
       \longmapsto(t_5,x_5,v_5)$$ is real analytic on the connected open set $(0,\infty)^5\times\mathbb R^{15}$, and has rank seven almost everywhere. In particular it is a submersion almost everywhere on the ordered region $0<t_5<\cdots<t_1<t_0$.*

*For convergent endpoint sequences with a nonaxial limiting velocity, convergent positive scale sequences, and the finite-temperature core formulas <a href="#nc:core-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:core-parameters">[nc:core-parameters]</a>, the analogous maps converge in $C^1$ on compact parameter sets to $\Phi$. Bounded measurable parameter densities supported in a common compact set therefore satisfy Lemma <a href="#nc:uniform-pullback" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:uniform-pullback">27</a>, including after any measurable spatial, velocity, or time restrictions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-five-gain-rank"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-five-gain-rank</summary>

*Proof.* For $t>0$, the derivative with respect to $u$ in the defining implicit equation is $$1+\frac{t\alpha\gamma}{\mu}
 S\bigl(\gamma(z-tu)/\mu\bigr)>0.$$ The implicit analytic function theorem gives an analytic profile for every $z\in\mathbb R$. Uniqueness, which also follows from this strict monotonicity, identifies the local profiles on overlaps. The recursive formulas <a href="#nc:rank-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:rank-map">[nc:rank-map]</a> are consequently analytic. They make sense even without ordering the positive times: a negative formal free-flight duration merely changes the point at which the next analytic profile is evaluated. This supplies the connected open domain stated in the lemma.

We exhibit one rank-seven point. Fix $t_5=\tau$ with $0<\tau<t_0$, and set all five collision times equal to $\tau$. After a rotation about the velocity axis, write $v_0=(R,0,z)$, $R>0$. All collision positions now coincide at $x_0-(t_0-\tau)v_0$, independently of the angular parameters. Thus write the five axial velocities simply as $p_i=a_i e_3$; their values $a_i$ may be arbitrary.

Choose $$k_1=(0,1,0),\qquad k_2=(1,-R,0).$$ They satisfy $k_1\perp n_1$, $k_2\perp n_2$, and span the transverse plane. Put $$w=\bar v_2=(R+1,1-R)\ne0,\qquad b_3=v_{2,3}-a_3,$$ and choose a sufficiently small nonzero number $\epsilon$ and $$k_3=\epsilon\left(-\frac{b_3w}{|w|^2},1\right).$$ Then $k_3\perp n_3$, its axial component is nonzero, and $\bar v_3\ne0$. Hence $k_1,k_2,k_3$ are linearly independent. Writing $\bar v_3=(X,Y)$, take $$k_4=(-Y,X,0),\qquad k_5=0.$$ The fourth increment is perpendicular to $n_4$, and $$\det(\bar n_4,\bar n_5)=X^2+Y^2>0.$$ Thus $n_4,n_5$ are nonparallel. All the normals in this construction are nonzero, and every chosen increment can be represented by $L_i=-n_i\times k_i/|n_i|^2$. Choose $L_5=0$.

At the equal-time configuration, $$x_5=x_0-v_0(t_0-t_1)
           -\sum_{i=1}^4v_i(t_i-t_{i+1}).$$ All derivatives of $x_5$ in angular directions vanish. Keeping $t_5$ fixed, its first three time columns are $$\partial_{t_1}x_5=-k_1,\qquad
 \partial_{t_2}x_5=-k_2,\qquad
 \partial_{t_3}x_5=-k_3.$$ Derivatives of the $v_i$, including derivatives of the position-dependent $p_i$, are multiplied by zero free-flight durations in this formula. In the final velocity block, $$\delta_{L_4}v_5=n_4\times\delta L_4,\qquad
 \delta_{L_5}v_5=n_5\times\delta L_5,$$ because $L_5=0$ and the $p_i$ have zero angular position derivatives at equal times. The sum of the planes $n_4^\perp+n_5^\perp$ is $\mathbb R^3$. Choose three angular columns spanning this sum. Together with the three time columns above, they give an invertible $6\times6$ block for $(x_5,v_5)$: its upper-right block is zero and both diagonal blocks are invertible. Appending the $t_5$ column and target coordinate gives a nonzero $7\times7$ minor.

This minor is a nontrivial analytic function on the connected domain. Its zero set has Lebesgue measure zero. For completeness, at any zero of a nontrivial analytic function some derivative of finite order is nonzero; otherwise its Taylor series vanishes on a neighborhood, and analytic continuation through overlapping balls in the connected domain makes the function identically zero. Choosing a derivative of minimal nonzero order puts the point on a regular zero set of a derivative of one lower order. There are countably many such derivatives, and each regular zero set is covered by countably many smooth hypersurface charts. Their union is null. Rank failure is contained in the zero set of our particular minor, so it is null as well. Continuity also shows directly that the witness persists at nearby strictly ordered times. It need not obey any spatial mask.

For the prelimit maps replace $p_i$ by <a href="#nc:core-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:core-parameters">[nc:core-parameters]</a> and include $w_i\in\mathbb R^3$ among the parameters. On compact sets the scales have positive lower bounds, $d_{r_n\mu_i}\to0$, and all implicit derivatives have the same positive lower bound one. Differentiating the implicit equations, or applying their implicit function formulas, gives $C^1$ convergence in the times, positions, and all bounded $L_i,w_i$. The limit is $\Phi$, independent of the fifteen spectator $w_i$-coordinates. Its rank is still seven almost everywhere on the enlarged parameter domain, by Fubini. Lemma <a href="#nc:uniform-pullback" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:uniform-pullback">27</a> now proves the final assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-nc-five-nonaxial-gains"></a>
<div id="nc:five-nonaxial-gains" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Vanishing of five nonaxial gains). *Suppose $\ell_n\to0$, the hit endpoints converge after periodic translation, and their limiting velocity is nonaxial. Suppose also that $0\le f_n\le2$ on $a\le t\le\tau_n$ and $f_n\to0$ in measure on every compact subset of $$(0,U)\times\mathbb R^3_x
       \times\{v\in\mathbb R^3:\bar v\ne0\}.$$ Then $$(\mathcal V_n^5f_n)(\tau_n,x_n,v_n)\longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-five-nonaxial-gains"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-five-nonaxial-gains</summary>

*Proof.* Fix an arbitrarily small error tolerance. Remove the tails of Lemma <a href="#nc:endpoint-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:endpoint-tightness">23</a>. All path velocities and positions are now in fixed bounded sets. At every encountered main core, $s\le Cr_n$, so its transverse width tends uniformly to zero.

Starting from a positive lower bound for the endpoint transverse speed, apply the nonaxial small-input estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-tightness">7</a> successively at gains $1,\ldots,5$. Choose positive numbers $b_1,\ldots,b_5$ so that deleting $|\bar v_i|<b_i$ costs less than the prescribed error at step $i$. At each step the previous lower bound has already been fixed; the later gain masses are bounded by <a href="#nc:iteration-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:iteration-norm">[nc:iteration-norm]</a>. Thus this is a finite successive choice, followed by $n\to\infty$. The sum of the five deleted contributions is arbitrarily small. Write $b>0$ for a lower bound on all retained transverse speeds, including $v_0$. For large $n$, every retained normal $v_{i-1}-p_i$ has length at least $b/2$.

Next delete collision points with $|y_i|<\rho$. Conditional on all earlier parameters, $y_i$ is an affine function of $t_i$ with derivative $\bar v_{i-1}$. Consequently $$\left|\{t_i:|y_i|<\rho\}\right|\le\frac{2\rho}{b}.$$ Integrate the current kernel using its mass bound $C/a$, and bound the later kernels by <a href="#nc:iteration-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:iteration-norm">[nc:iteration-norm]</a>. Summing over the five steps bounds this deletion by $C_a\rho/b$. Choose $\rho>0$ after the speed thresholds. The retained scales now belong to a compact subinterval $[\mu_-,\mu_+]\subset(0,\infty)$. Mesh convergence gives a fixed finite list of scales on this interval, with positive limits, after an immaterial enlargement of its endpoints. There are consequently only finitely many five-tuples of scales and signs to consider.

<a id="nc:upper-endpoint-cost"></a>
The tested time $t_5$ must remain inside the weak-limit interval. If $U>1$, it already lies in the compact interval $[a,1]\Subset(0,U)$. If $U=1$, delete $t_5>1-\delta$. Ordering forces all five times into $(1-\delta,\tau_n)$, whose length is at most $\delta$. This costs at most $$\label{nc:upper-endpoint-cost}
 2(C/a)^5\frac{\delta^5}{5!}.$$ Choose $\delta>0$ to make this as small as desired. No convergence at the event time is being asserted.

Fix one retained scale/sign tuple. Drop all spatial masks in constructing its maps, and retain the masks in its nonnegative integrand. Each plane increment is bounded, all normals are bounded above and below, and each cold parameter is bounded. Apply Lemma <a href="#nc:plane-parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:plane-parameters">26</a> successively. The integral becomes an integral over five times, fifteen $L$-coordinates, and fifteen cold parameter coordinates. Its support lies in a fixed compact subset of $(0,\infty)^5\times\mathbb R^{30}$, and its density is uniformly bounded: the core amplitudes are at most $C/a$, the factors $|n_i|\zeta(L_i\cdot n_i/|n_i|)$ are bounded, and all retained ratios $H(v_i)/H(v_{i-1})$ are bounded. The masks, time ordering, and cut conditions are arbitrary measurable factors between zero and one in this density.

The target $(t_5,x_5,v_5)$ on the actual support lies in a fixed compact set $B$ inside the open weak-limit region, away from the velocity axis. Set $F_n=f_n\mathbf 1_B\mathbf 1_{\{a\le t\le\tau_n\}}$ and extend it by zero; it is bounded by two and tends to zero in measure. The maps converge in $C^1$ to the map in Lemma <a href="#lem:five-gain-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:five-gain-rank">28</a>. That lemma and Lemma <a href="#nc:uniform-pullback" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:uniform-pullback">27</a> show that the integral for this tuple tends to zero. Notice that the entire parameter compact need not map into $B$; only the support of the measurable density must do so.

There are finitely many tuples, so their sum tends to zero. The total limsup of the original integral is bounded by the arbitrarily small errors from the preceding deletions. Letting those tolerances tend to zero proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Completion of the nonvanishing argument

<details id="proof-stmt-nc-five-nonaxial-gains-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-nc-five-nonaxial-gains</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:nonzero-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:nonzero-limit">21</a>.* Assume $l=0$ and $g=0$, as in the reduction above. Choose endpoints $(x_n,v_n)$ at time $\tau_n$ in the common full-measure set fixed above such that $f_n(\tau_n,x_n,v_n)\ge1/2$. For each fixed $a$, the residual in <a href="#nc:five-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:five-residual">[nc:five-residual]</a> tends to zero along any subsequence of these endpoints. Indeed, Lemma <a href="#nc:endpoint-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:endpoint-tightness">23</a> first excludes large velocities and escaping positions. Otherwise choose convergent endpoints after a periodic translation. If the limiting velocity is axial, the first two gains vanish by Lemma <a href="#nc:two-axial-gains" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:two-axial-gains">25</a>, and the last three have bounded mass by <a href="#nc:iteration-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:iteration-norm">[nc:iteration-norm]</a>. If it is nonaxial, use <a href="#nc:zero-in-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:zero-in-measure">[nc:zero-in-measure]</a> in Lemma <a href="#nc:five-nonaxial-gains" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nc:five-nonaxial-gains">29</a>. This covers every subsequence; hence the residual tends to zero for the original endpoint sequence as well.

Taking the limsup in <a href="#nc:five-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:five-residual">[nc:five-residual]</a> at these endpoints gives $$\frac12
 \le a\sum_{j=0}^4\frac{(C\log(1/a))^j}{j!}.$$ Finally let $a\downarrow0$. The right side tends to zero, a contradiction. Throughout this argument $U$ was allowed to be infinite, and the case $U=1$ was handled by <a href="#nc:upper-endpoint-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nc:upper-endpoint-cost">[nc:upper-endpoint-cost]</a>. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:branching"></a>
### Branching growth and the dilated horizon

We now choose the parameters left free in the construction. Their choice will be independent of the subsequence in Proposition <a href="#prop:blowup-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blowup-limit">20</a>, including the phase of its geometric mesh and its lower endpoint $l\in[0,1]$.

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-dilated-horizon"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-dilated-horizon" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30**. *There are fixed positive choices of $\eta,\alpha,\gamma,K$, with $\eta,\alpha,\gamma$ small and $K$ large, for which every limit in Propositions <a href="#prop:blowup-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blowup-limit">20</a> and <a href="#prop:nonzero-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:nonzero-limit">21</a> has $U<\infty$. These parameters can be fixed before the final choices of $s_0,d_0,T_0$. The same choices work for every limiting mesh and every $l\in[0,1]$.*

</div>

The proof uses only positive, finite collision histories starting at a strictly positive time. We first establish estimates in a constant medium.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Positive histories and their normalization

<a id="br:sphere-map"></a>
<a id="br:gain-mass"></a>
For $v,w\in\mathbb R^3$ and $n\in S^2$, set $$\label{br:sphere-map}
\Phi(v,w,n)=\frac{v+w}{2}+\frac{|v-w|}{2}n,
\qquad \,\mathrm d\varsigma(n)=\frac{\,\mathrm dn}{4\pi}.$$ Thus $\varsigma$ is a probability measure. By Lemma <a href="#lem:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:carleman">3</a>, a collision with incident velocities $v,w$ has rate $2\pi|v-w|$, and the marginal law of either child is the pushforward of $\varsigma$ under $\Phi(v,w,\cdot)$. The two marginal laws agree because $n\mapsto-n$ preserves $\varsigma$. Consequently, for a nonnegative velocity measure $B$ and a density $f$, $$\label{br:gain-mass}
\int_{\mathbb R^3}P(B,f)(v)\,\mathrm dv=\int_{\mathbb R^3}\nu_B(v)f(v)\,\mathrm dv.$$ The coefficient $2$ in the gain of <a href="#eq:limit-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-equation">[eq:limit-equation]</a> therefore counts both children, while the loss removes their parent once.

<a id="br:history-weight"></a>
More generally, fix Borel representatives of the coefficients of a two-beam medium $$\widetilde B(t,x)=\sum_{\sigma=\pm1}b_\sigma(t,x)
                  \delta_{w_\sigma(t,x)},\qquad
q_\sigma(t,x,v)=2\pi b_\sigma(t,x)|v-w_\sigma(t,x)|,
\qquad \nu=\sum_\sigma q_\sigma.$$ All pointwise path conditions below refer to these representatives. On $[a,b]$, a history with ordered collision times $a<\tau_1<\cdots<\tau_j<b$, signs $\sigma_i$, and directions $n_i$ follows free flights between collisions and uses $$V_{\tau_i}=\Phi(V_{\tau_i-},w_{\sigma_i}(\tau_i,X_{\tau_i}),n_i).$$ Its weight relative to $\prod_i\,\mathrm d\tau_i\,\mathrm d\varsigma(n_i)$ is $$\label{br:history-weight}
\exp\left(-\int_a^b\nu(s,X_s,V_s)\,\mathrm ds\right)
\prod_{i=1}^j2q_{\sigma_i}(\tau_i,X_{\tau_i},V_{\tau_i-}).$$ For $j=0$ the empty product is one.

<a id="stmt-br-positive-histories"></a>
<div id="br:positive-histories" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31**. *Suppose $f\ge0$ solves $D_tf=2P(\widetilde B,f)-\nu f$ in the local transport sense. On bounded positive-time regions where the coefficients and collision terms are integrable, start with any nonnegative portion of $f(a)$. Integration of any collection of bounded finite histories with weights <a href="#br:history-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:history-weight">[br:history-weight]</a> gives a measure dominated by $f(b)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-br-positive-histories"><summary>Proof of stmt-br-positive-histories</summary>

*Proof.* Set $G=2P(\widetilde B,f)$. In free coordinates $f^\sharp(t,x,v)=f(t,x+tv,v)$, the equation becomes $$\partial_t f^\sharp=(G-\nu f)^\sharp.$$ On bounded tubes its right side is locally integrable, so $f^\sharp$ has absolutely continuous time sections for almost every $(x,v)$ and local $L^1$ time traces. Fubini’s theorem also makes $\nu^\sharp$ integrable on almost every bounded characteristic segment. The one-dimensional integrating-factor formula therefore consists of the freely transported initial density multiplied by its survival factor, plus the time integral of the nonnegative gain with the same survival factor. Repeated substitution into the gain gives <a href="#br:history-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:history-weight">[br:history-weight]</a>, by <a href="#br:sphere-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:sphere-map">[br:sphere-map]</a> and <a href="#br:gain-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:gain-mass">[br:gain-mass]</a>. Every remainder is nonnegative. Restricting the histories, or the initial density, preserves the lower bound. Tonelli’s theorem justifies the substitutions. On bounded portions with total gain rate at most $C$, the $j$-collision term is bounded by $C^j(b-a)^j/j!$ times the initial mass.

These formulas apply to point-velocity partners. Indeed Lemma <a href="#lem:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:carleman">3</a> gives a density for the gain against each such partner, with mass <a href="#br:gain-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:gain-mass">[br:gain-mass]</a>. Thus free flight and each gain preserve absolute continuity when the starting measure has a density. Null spatial sets in representatives of the coefficients do not affect the integrated formula. ◻

</details>

<a id="br:global-rate"></a>
For the background <a href="#eq:background" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:background">[eq:background]</a>, $|u_\sigma|\le1+\alpha$ and $$\label{br:global-rate}
0\le b_\sigma(t,x)\le\frac{\theta K}{t\alpha\gamma},
\qquad
\nu_B(t,x,v)\le\frac{C(1+|v|)}{t}\quad(t>0).$$ At each mesh interface choose the value from the outer adjacent annulus, and on the transverse axis set the medium to zero. These are Borel representatives obeying the same bounds. All bounded finite flights starting at positive time have strictly positive survival. The cap and Lemma <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> give locally integrable collision terms. The local transport formula in Proposition <a href="#prop:blowup-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blowup-limit">20</a> therefore supplies time traces of $g$ on bounded regions. Testing nonnegative compact functions and taking a time limit shows that $g(t)\le(t+l)H$ also holds at these traces. We use this representative.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### A reference flight with a positive potential

We convert the branching history weights into expectations along a single Markov flight with a positive potential. This is a jump-process Feynman–Kac representation, related to the many-to-one formulas for branching systems; see (Harris and Roberts 2017, sec. 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[HarrisRoberts2017](#ref-HarrisRoberts2017)] for general probabilistic context. The finite-history calculation below proves the precise representation for the present collision law.

Fix $$D=5,\qquad E=\{v:|v|\le D\},\qquad
B_0=\delta_{e_3}+\delta_{-e_3},\qquad
\nu_0(v)=2\pi\bigl(|v-e_3|+|v+e_3|\bigr).$$ Let $J(v,\,\mathrm dv')$ be the single-child law obtained by choosing a sign with probabilities proportional to $|v-\sigma e_3|$ and then using <a href="#br:sphere-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:sphere-map">[br:sphere-map]</a>. Put $p(v)=J(v,E)$.

<a id="stmt-br-retention"></a>
<div id="br:retention" class="lemma">

<a id="br:potential-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 32**. *For every $v\in E$, $p(v)\ge25/26$. The reference history sum restricted to velocities in $E$ is represented by a Markov flight with velocity jump rate $\lambda(v)=2p(v)\nu_0(v)$, jump law $J(v,\,\mathrm dv')\mathbf 1_E(v')/p(v)$, and multiplicative weight $$\exp\left(\int_0^A \mathcal W(V_s)\,\mathrm ds\right),
\qquad \mathcal W(v)=(2p(v)-1)\nu_0(v).$$ One may take $$\label{br:potential-bounds}
0<c_0=\frac{48\pi}{13}\le \mathcal W(v),\qquad
\lambda(v)\le C_0=48\pi\quad(v\in E).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-br-retention"><summary>Proof of stmt-br-retention</summary>

*Proof.* Set $s=|v|^2$, $z=v_3$, and $R=\sqrt{(s+1)^2-4z^2}$. For either partner sign the squared child speed has the law $$|v'|^2=\frac{s+1+RZ}{2},
\qquad Z\text{ uniform on }[-1,1].$$ This follows by expanding the square in <a href="#br:sphere-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:sphere-map">[br:sphere-map]</a>: a coordinate of a uniform point on $S^2$ is uniform on $[-1,1]$. The maximum is at most $s+1$, so there is no exit for $s\le24$. For $24<s\le25$, the exit probability, if positive, is $$\frac12+\frac{s-49}{2R}
\le\frac12+\frac{s-49}{2(s+1)}
=\frac{s-24}{s+1}\le\frac1{26}.$$ The inequality uses $R\le s+1$ and $s-49<0$. If $R=0$, the squared speed is deterministic and at most 25. Mixing the signs preserves the bound.

A Markov history of rate $\lambda$ has the survival factor $e^{-\int\lambda}$, and one rate times jump-law factor per collision. Multiplication by $e^{\int(\lambda-\nu_0)}$ changes this exactly to <a href="#br:history-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:history-weight">[br:history-weight]</a>, restricted to $E$. Both history sums converge absolutely on finite intervals because their rates are bounded. Finally $4\pi\le\nu_0(v)\le24\pi$ on $E$, and $2p(v)-1\ge12/13$, giving <a href="#br:potential-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="br:potential-bounds">[br:potential-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Three collisions give a volume minorization

Write $P_t(v,\cdot)$ for the transition law of the preceding velocity process. We establish a Doeblin minorization, the classical common-mass condition for mixing of Markov chains (Doblin 1940) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Doeblin1940](#ref-Doeblin1940)]. The three-collision geometry proving this condition and the resulting contraction argument are both given below.

<a id="stmt-br-minorization"></a>
<div id="br:minorization" class="lemma">

<a id="br:doeblin"></a>
**Lemma 33**. *There are $r_0>0$ and $\varepsilon_0>0$ such that $$\label{br:doeblin}
P_1(v,A)\ge\varepsilon_0|A\cap B(0,r_0)|\quad(v\in E)$$ for every Borel set $A\subset E$. For each $\zeta>0$, there is also $\varepsilon_\zeta>0$ such that $$P_1(v,B(e_1,\zeta)\cap\{|v'|<D\})\ge\varepsilon_\zeta\quad(v\in E).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-br-minorization"><summary>Proof of stmt-br-minorization</summary>
